WOMEN'S CHAT.

and the

The Duchess of Teck.—ln the Princess of Wales, the Duchess of Teel; and Princess Alexander of Teck, have found a devoted friend and sister-in-law, and the chil- dren of the former spend much of their time with the Princess Mary and the Princes of Wales. The Duchess of Teck—then Lady Margaret Grosvenor, was married to the eldest of the Princess of Wales' brothers in 1894, and her wedding from Eaton Hall made a memorable event in the annals of Cheshire. The Duchess has no radiant beauty, but she possesses a charming expression, and most fascinating manners, also the" Grosvenor voice" which is soft and low, with a clear tone and a delicate pronunciation. The Duchess of Teck, like Lady Arthur Grosvenor; has a great affection for the "simple life," preferring a pony trap to a State Coach, and a cotton frock to the most exquisite Court gown. Women's Emancipation Among Oriental Peoples.--An indication that the Women's Movement is not confined to Western civilisation is afforded by the announce- ment than an Egyptian girl student has taken her 8.A., degree. She is the first Moslem girl to gain this dis- tinction, but there are now some six or seven govern- ment schools for native girls in Egypt, and more attention is being paid to female education. In all directions the position of women is steadily improving among the Oriental people; The fact that there is in Pekin a Chinese daily newspaper devotedly exclusively to women's interests shows the world-wide character of the feminine awakening. In the neighbouring country of Siam, - whose ruler King Chulalongyorn was our recent visitor, the position of women is in no sense one of subjection. Their robust physique. and erect bear- ing, together with a similarity of dress, often cause a stranger to mistake them for men, and they enjoy a large measure of freedom and equality in Social and domestic life. Thei Countess of Jersey.—Lady Jersey, whose garden parties at Osterley Park are again this year so great a feature of the London season, is a regular globe-trotter, having visited such remote places as New Caledonia and Samoa, and gone much out of the beaten track in China, Japan, and other Eastern lands. In her beautiful country home, Osterley Park, near Isleworth, however, she finds her greatest pleasure. The grounds, noted for their red rambler roses, and masses of rhododendrons, are magnificent, and the house is a perfect example of the Adams style of architecture. In its lovely rooms are many wonderful treasures in china, silver, and tipestry, the pictures too are pricelessiand in the long picture gallery hangs the historic equestrian pot trait of Charles 1., by Vandyke. The Countess of Jersey, nth) Miss Margaret Elizabeth Leigh, has Tudor blood in her veins, as she traces her descent on the distaff side from Mary sister of Henry VIII. This great lady is clever, exceedingly well-read, a good linguist, and one of the finest orators in this or any other country. French Marriage Laws.—ln France a change has just been effected in the marriage laws, which is of consider- able importance to the French people. Hitherto no Frenchman of whatever age, cogld marry without his parents' consent. It is true that after the age of twenty- five, he could apply to the Courts to decide whether this was unreasonably withheld, but, as most people naturally shrink from the publicity attending such a course, it is very seldom resorted to. Now under the new law, any man or woman over the age of thirty, may marry without the permission of parents, or even notifying the family of the event. There have been instances of couples waiting until they were far advanced in years, for the death of a parent who had refused sanction of their marriage, but the new statute will give entire freedom in such matters after the age of thirty. The Value of Cheerfulness.—Cheerfrilness is a virtue we should all try to cultivate, and it is therefore with some alarm that we read that strawberries make people irritable and bad-tempered. Whether there is any truth in this, or whether it is merely a theory of the faddists. it is a melancholy fact that a great many people do not seem to want the assistance of strawberries or anything half so nice to make them gloomy and miserable. They prefer to live in the shadows. Yet it is the cheerful woman who succeeds in life. As a teacher it is the sunny disposition that instinctively attracts the young I toward 3 her, and makes them do their hest to please her, and in all phases of business life it is the cheerfully I disposed who lead the way. No doubt it is a great, extent a gift of the gods,but it is also true that many with external advantages seem to make no effort to subdue the feminine jealousies, tempers and .pettishness that one never finds in the sweet-tempered and kindly dis- posed towards humanity. Luggage Thefts.—Many advantages are claimed for corridor railway carriages, but they afford the criminal rather more than the usual opportunities for luggage. It would be well to bear this fact in mind now that the holiday travelling season is at hand. Well-dressed thieves can pass up and down a corridor train, and are much less likely to be noticed in the act of removing unguarded luggage than is the case when the exit opens direct on the platform, where, in all nrobabilify, the owner is saying good-bye to friends. In the case of a separate compartment train the theft would he noticed, but it is much less likely to be discovered in time when the thief can emerge far down the platform and dis- appear among the crowd. No doubt the corridor carriage affords one a larger measure of personal safety, but even this carmot always he taken for granted. If it placed us more within reach of help, it affords the maniac or criminal easy access, and unless each com- partment were occupied, it would be quite possible to be caught in a defenceless position. A New Trimming.—Straw ribbon is an innovation in the world of millinery, and among purchasers of travelling headgear, is likely to find a certain amonet, or favour this summer. The effect of the "ribbon" is that of peveral straws in different shades, joined together by means of a species of tapestry embroidery. For more dressy "creations" the immense bow or lace, which is well-wired an caught in the centre with ma elaborate buckle, or a huge single rose, is having a great vogue, and the notion will Le especially welcome to the girl or woman who happens to baveashort length of good lace, that has perchance seen its best days, nod yet promises to lend itself successfully to the wiring process. Chenille Boleros.—Our old and delightful friend chenille, is appearing in yet another guise, namely knitted to form little sleeveless bolero, - which are finished off all round with a tiny fringe, or halls of the chenille. These dainty garments are made up over a

Inexpensive yet Effective Trinkets.—Mother-of-pearl necklaces are much worn by young girls this season the heads—generally of irregular shapes and sizes—being separated by crystal beads, which helps to enhance the brilliance of the necklet. By a clever process, the pearl is now coloured in a great variety of shades, so that not only are the opalescent blue, white, and rose of the natural mother-of-pearl available, but such hues as rich orange, a glowing pink, a lovely mauve, and a translucent blue. These pretty pecklaces have lately found much favour in the eyes of bridegrooms as suit- able gifts to the bride's train of "maids,' Ainateur Photqgraphy.—Amateurs bent upon taking full-length photographs of their friends would do well to bear in mind the fact that the sitting-posture is the best for the victim. The standing pose is rarely a success, never, one mipt say' unless the martyr has ' some knowledge of physical culture In order to stand , easily, with the correct pose of the body, the muscles • being well in hand, though not tense. The three- quarter pose, beloved of photograpners a decade or so ago, is seldom used now by experts in the art. Another point the amateur photographer must carefully -observe is the concealment of the feet, and yet another the placing of the hands. They must never be held forward on the knees, or they will "come out" several sizes . too large, and spoil the picture. The side view of the hands is the best before the camera, when the effect will be pretty and tapering.

V/IRRAL SHOW.

Local Prize Winners.

I The annual show in connection with the Wirral • Agricultural Society was held at Bebington on Wednes- day and Thursday and being favoured with generally fine wcather was largely attended, many thousands of people passing through the turnstiles. The arrange- ments generally followed the lines of recent years. . Recognising the value of a good impression, upon entering a floral display greeted the eyes of visitors, while at the head of the central avenue and shielding . the ring was a profusion of shrubs, the general effect being one highly pleasing. As displaying the substantial I growth of the society, it will suffice to state that the entries steadily rose from 1,567 in 1886 to the record of 6,260 in 1902, the sharp decline which followed, owing to the visitations of storm, having since been arrested, so that the tendency is again upward. The decreases this year, as compared with last year, are on horses, pigs, and pigeons, while there are increases in dogs, poultry, cheese and butter. The total entries numbered 4,448 as against 4;524 last year. In the pig section the chief winners were Sir Gilbert Oreenall, Aquilla Dewhurst (Bradford), J. Jefferson (Peel Hall, Chester), W. H. and E.Wherry (Bourne),and T. Dodd and Sons (Mollington). Amon,g•the horses, pride of place belonged to the heavy classes, the cart horses once more impressing the enormous improvement in breed which has been wrought 1 during the last decade. .Tames Gould, of Lymm, was 1 successful in winning outright two of the champion cups—one for stallions and anntker for mares. Another remarkable success was that of A. C. Sparkes (Altrinc- ham), who won the 50-guinea cup, and has promised to ei-e the society another to take its place. The following are the local prize winners : PIGS (OPEN COMPETITION).

Large Whire Boar, not exceeding 12 months.-2, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. Walton Hall, Warrington. Large White Sow, not exceeding 12 months.-3, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. The Examihen, Satulday, July 13, 1907

t WOMAN'S REALM.

I IZIGHTS RESERVED.] FLOTSAM itilD JETSAM.

BY JEAN MIDDLEMASS,

Author of "A Woman's Calvary," "A Life Surrender," "The iollow Badge," "1:li Lawful Wife," "Who Loses Pays," &e.

"How shall I ever tell him—how shall I avez dare to tell him!"

Mrs. Smallwood; while she spoke, lifted up a golden-haired boy who hnd been thrown down by a too rapid motor-ear, and lwld him in her arms with an amount of anxiety and loving care with which none of the neighb.ouus had believed that she was possessed. "Perhaps he ain't much hurt," observed a bystander. "Children most always come off scot- free."

"Oh, I don't know—.l don't know—poor mite ! " almost sobbed Mrs. Smallwood, as she pushed through the gathering crowd with her little burden, who neither cried nor moved, only by an occasional low whimper shewing that he was still alive.

Mrs. Smallwood was the landlady of a small Bloomsbury lodging-house. She carried the child up to the top fioor,and, kicking a door open with her foot, laid him on a pallet bed in a barely- furnished room with a northern light, which Harold Fyfe called his studio; though there was little of the studio about it save a pinchbeck mixture of art and poverty. No cosiness, no pretty objects to please the artistic eye with their beauty,. nothing to relieve the angles of poverty and want.

Yet good work was done there, for Harold Fyfe had been touched by the wing of genius' and was no sluggard. His miniatures were little gems. If only connoisseurs could see them, and sitters come -who had wealth at command! "But in good time Dame Fortune would look in at the open door "—so he, being somewhat of an optimist, thought and hoped. Meanwhile, utterly friendless, he struggled bravely against want, even at times starvation; and there was no one to help. His landlady would occasionally do him some small kindness in a disagreeable way; but she was an ill-mannered woman, who in- variably sought to hide her good deeds and kindly thoughts under the impenetrable veil of hardness and fault-ending; and assuredly, being a practical woman,the did find fault with Harold Fyfe, and,as she told the few cronies with whom she associated, not without reason—hence their surprise to-day. "Here was this young fellow," she was wont to say, "scarcely out of long clothes, with ne'er a relation to guide him, and not a sixpence he didn't earn with his brush, a-saddlin' of himself with _another man's child! She didn't hold with such foolishness; not but what little Jo was a sweet wee chap,and a beauty—look at his picture, done over and over again, hanging on them bare walls; still, he ought to have been sent to the workus. But there, she couldn't ha' done it her- self, so she supposed she Must let Mr. Fyfe take his own way for a bit longer—p'rhaps it would all come right ! " Mrs. Smallwood's orations always ended in half coming round and then doing something to help "her boys," as she called Harold Fyfe and little Jo.

Not that what she said or thought on the matter affected Harold in the very least. Even if she had turned him into the street he would not have parted from Jo, who was the very light of his life.

It was for Jo's sake he longed for the orders and riches which were so long in coming; for his sake that he often went without the food that would have brought colour to his pale cheeks. The little one had never yet gone without his daily meals, and never should, if Harold could help it. In truth, as Mrs. Smallwood said, Jo was a little beauty, dainty in limb, refined in feature, vyith golden hair that rippled in curls over his shoulders quite "out of drawing" in so poor a neighbourhood as Mitcham-street, Bloomsbury. Jo was the one ray of gladness in Harold Fyfe's poverty-stricken home—the being in whom all his hopes were centred—yet Jo was a mere waif. He had no family, no belongings—mere flotsam and jetsam they both were, tossed up from the depths of life's stream to struggle on its surface as best they could. Jo was only five, and during four years he had known no home but that provided by the Fyfes. It was Harold's mother who had taken Jo when he was a child of a few months old, with the expectation of a rich return, since his parents were seemingly people of means, who, merely gaing to America to look after some property, would pay Mrs. Fyfe liberally for the motherly care she would take of their little one during their absence, which would scarcely be for more than twelve months.

A year's money in adyance was given to her— money which was speedily spent in providing tuition for Harold's budding talent in portraiture —then came silence. Months passed into years, and the American wanderers were heard ot no more. Mrs. Fyfe died, and little Jo was left as a legacy to the penniless Harold. "Send him to the workhouse! No, never, he loved him far too well; besides,what good fortune would he obtain in this world, what chance in the great Hereafter, if he wantonly parted with little Jo ?" This was Harold's invariable answer when it was suggested by acquaintances that he should get rid of the child.

And now? • Now, as Mrs. Smallwood laid her tiny burden on the bed, was it possible that the Gordian knot of trouble and difficulty had been cut, and the little boy had been summoned to a brighter Home? Not yet—not yet. Still, there is Life for little Jo.

Harold had sprung up from his painting, con- stornated by Mrs. Smallwood's sudden entrance. As he leant over the child, his face whiter than that of the prostrate boy, Jo opened his eyes. "Rollie," he said, holding up his mouth to be kissed and calling Harold by his pet name. He was not dead, and, much to the surprise of the aggrieved Mrs. Smallwood, not even hurt, only frightened; for aggrieved she assuredly was, partly because there was no catastrophe to gloat over, but chiefly because she had been scared into shewincr an amount of feeling that was quite foreign to her nature. ,And now she supposed it would all be just as it was before—only more so, for little Jo would be more petted and spoilt than ever by that silly Mr. Fyfe. Labouring under the reaction of finding Jo was not killed, Mrs. Smallwood was resuming her old grumbles, but not much time was given for their circulation before another excitement awaited her.

On the threshold there was an unexpected vision.

It was the child's voice that first drew their attention.

"Oh, the pretty lady !""he exclaimed. He was right. Fair in feature, svelte in form, Annette Lascelles was a pieture. Harold Fyfe's first thought was that her face exhibited in a miniature would make his fortune, his second was that she was too lovely, too sweet. to be shewn br her eighteen summers to the public gaze. "You will forgive me for intruding—but the poor little boy, I felt I must ask if he was hurt," she said, shyly. • "Kind lady.. Jo loves you," murmured the boy. She stooped and kissed him ;, then she looked round, as though wondering to whom he belonged. Harold by this time had found his voice, and thanked her timidly. Mrs. Smallwood only stared in blank wonderment as to what would happen next. And so she well might, for events were only at the outset. From Jo's face' to Harold's, and then round the room Annette Laseelles' eyes wandered. Mrs. room, appeared to be altogether left out of the picture. "I was so sorry—so very sorry it should have nappened—not my fault, but Louis's. Since the dear child is not hurt it does not, however, matter so much, though a fright is always horrid--Ah! you paint. How delightful!" ..

She went forward to where hem had been sitting, and looked at his work with much know- ledgeable appreciation. He meanwhile stood staring as one transfixed. That such a radiant being should visit his poor little studio was most overpowering.

It- seemed- as. if his prayers and hopes were about to be realised through the keeping of Baby Jo—and so, indeed, they were. She wandered mind the tiny room looking at

the many portraits of Jo that graced the walls, and making many laudatory, remarks, till at last Harold, "screwing his courage to the sticking point," said: _ _ _ _ __ _

" If I might only be allowed to make 3 ministaw you!" "A rare pretty one it would be—a gladsom face ,and a clever artist," interpolated Mrs Smallwood, evidently to the visitor's amusement for she coloured up and laughed as she said cheerily: "More unlikely things have happened. Yo • must come and see my father's collection o. miniatures. He has several Cosways, as well a other gems.. In fact, he is well known as

collect-or—Sir John Laseelles is my fathet Perhaps you have heard of him ? " "Laseelles ? " murmured Harold Fyfe—"Las- celles ? The name is very familiar," and while he spoke he was evidently searching in his mind for some connecting link. Miss Laseelles saw it and said, graciously: "I hope it will become more familiar. May I ask your name—with which, perhaps, my father is acquainted ? " !Harold Fyfe—but I am quite unknown." _

"I do not think you will lave to say that very long," she said. "You will come to 101, Lowndes-street and see my father's pictures, will you 'not? Perhaps orders will follow. Please bring Jo with you." There was an amount of manner and aplomb about this young lady which was rather in advance of her eighteen years, the explanation being that she had kept her father's house and been his constant companion ever since she was fifteen.

When she left the Mitcham-street quarters it seemed to Harold as if she had taken the sun- shine with her, but then she had also taken his promise that he would go to Lowndes-street on the morrow, and in looking forward to re-meeting he was comforted; while as for Jo and Mrs. Smallwood they were both so excited that it had been difficult to decide which was the more so of thA two.

No more work was done that day. Harold Fyfe wandered around like a man in a dream, while Mrs. Smallwood furbished up Jo's scanty ward- robe, supplementing it out of her own stores till she succeeded in making him look the perfect picture that in her heart she believed him to be. Nor was practical Mrs. Smallwood's care objectless. "If these rich people would only take the charge of Jo and relieve Mr. Fyfe of the burden, she would in future always look on a runaway motor-car as a blessing," she told her friends. "She had been a fool not to let the young lady know how matters stood between Mr. Fyfe and the child; but, sure as her name was Sarah Small- wood, she'd brill.- a little helli about from that there quarter, that she would!" If Harold had only known what his landlady's busy brain was planning he would not. have thanked her so cordially for the care she had bestowed on Jo's toilet, or have gone: off to Lowndes-street feeling as light-hearted as if he were already a well-known miniaturist, who could_command his guineas. In what a new atmosphere was he about to plunge! The Lascelles' home was typical for luxury and elegance. The soft yielding of the Aubusson carpets, the magnificence of the decorations, the grave, silent attention of the servants, the reflection of light, cleverly cast by many mirrors on the superb works of art that covered the walls, made this young fellow from the attic in Mitcham-street look around him in stupefied wonder, while the mixture of torturing timidity and delicious sensuousness that came over him was a phase of feeling he had never before experienced. Annette, too—her beauty, her grace, as she, the chatelaine, did the honours of the house, with her courteous father!

All contributed to make Harold Fyfe's pulses quicken. Yet while Annette tactful and thoughtful, fed little Jo with bonbons till the young artist should have time to become more at home with his surroundings, a memory from his own childish days seemed to come to him—a recollection that such a, luxurious home as that in which he now found himself was not wholly unknown to him.

In the far-off long ago the Fyfes, as the Lascelles did to-day, held their heads above the common herd of mankind, and glorying in their possessions dwelt in luxurious ease. But their riches had passed into other hands, and before even Harold was the age of tiny Jo, starvation and misery was ever their refrain. But, thanks to the motor-car accident, a page had been turned over in the book of Fate. Harold would be permitted once more to experi- ence, the mysterious influence of comfort and beauty. Sir John Lascelles was not a man who ever did: things by halves. He gave Harold, rent free, the use of a studio he owned and which was at present in want of a tenant, and to the new studio sitters came in admiring swarms, till in the course of a very few weeks the erst starving Harold found himself the fashion; while as for Mrs. Smallwood, now become his housekeeper, there was no need for her to make any suggestion to Miss Lascelles about the future of little Jo. , Now that there was money to feed and dress him Mrs. Smallwood was as selfishly anxious to keep him as she had once been that he should be sent away; still, in her practical mind she could not help occasionally wondering to what the present state of affairs would lead.

It was Sir John who, shortsighted, as many another father has been before him, surveyed with pleasure the new era in Harold's life to which he himself had so largely contributed, without looking forward to the result which every new departure produces. The scales fell from his eyes so suddenly that the blazing light thus revealed was staggering. They were together when he entered the studio; his arm—Harold's—was round Annette's waist, and she, looking up into his face with large, loving blue eyes, was suffering the embrace—gladly. A pauper a mere dauber, whom he had virtually picked out of the gutter and placed in positions to support himself and that nameless brat—to dare to presume to make love to his daughter! Great powers! it was beyond the limits of presumption and ingratitude. She, too—lvhat could the girl be thinking of to let the fellow thus forget himself? But he would not allow it—he would resent it to the full. And he did.

The vials of his wrath descended copiously on the heads of the two surprised young people. it nnette was sent back to Lowndes-street in absolute disgrace, and told that, at the peril of incurring such displeasure as would result in her being turned out of his house, she was never to enter the studio again—while as for Harold he had done with him. A man, who could so out- rage all the law § of gratitude and even of self- respect, was so totally unworthy, that the sooner he returned to the mire from where he had sprung the better for all concerned. In vain Annette pleaded, and tears flowed unrestrainedly; in vain Haroldlassured Sir John that though he had dared much in loving his daughter, yet the daring was not fraught with the dishonour be supposed. Sir John would hear nothing. Neither reason- ing nor tears availed. He was absolutely inexor- able. The Iyotteg people were parted •irrevocably, Annette to pass her time in such pining and weeping that like "a worm i' the bud they fed on 'her damask cheek." Yet Sir John, as he saw the effect this separation from Harold had on his much-loved child, only vituperated the more severely against the young painter. Never- theless, .he was moved by it, though he did not apparently relent.

Harold meantime, as Sir John had perhaps expected would be the ease, was toppling over from the pedestal to which he had been raised, not because orders and sitters failed, but because he no longer had the energy to work, but passed long hours in gazing at Annette's face as he iiimself had reproduced it on ivory, and in lamenting the absence of his lost love. In vain worthy Mrs. Smallwood inserted thP sharp needle of upbraiding that should have pricked him into action —he needed neither pricks nor stings. It was little jo who once more saved the situation.

"Who is this rr. Fyfe about whom so many people are talking ? " asked a lady who was lunching with the Laseelles. "I knew some Fyfes once—to a good old family they belonged. Mrs. Fyfe,with her white hair and benevolent manners, was quite charming." •-There is no Mrs. Fyfe. She is dead." It was Sir John who answered. Annette was rendered speechless by the seemingly random shot that struck so painfully home. "Au!" sighed the lady; and she repeated the one word "dead."

Sho looked almost as munh overcome as wav

Annette, which somewhat surprised and annoyed Sir John. Quickly, however, she pulled herself together, and said in a casual tone:

'l'l have been so long abroad, I find so many of my friends have passed away. I wonder if this artist is any relation to the dear old Mrs. Fyfe I WU* to know. Will YOU COMB with me to his studio, miss Laseeltes r I snould rather like to see him, and I am too shy to go alone."

There was an awkward silence for a second. Annette was white as ashes. Again it was Sir John who answered, after a quick look from one to the other of the two ladies. What prompted him to speak as he did he never knew. Certainly it was not reason, it must have been some mysierious, uncontrollable impulse. He was naturally not a little impulsive—nevertheless his reply caused such a revulsion of feeling in Annette, that it was with, difficulty she prevented herself from crying out. "I do not approvp that my daughter should visit young artists' studio's, Mrs. La Porte, but that' she should accompany you, of course I can have no objection. You will, I -know, look after her and see that everything is en regle." Mrs. La Porte, who was a charming widow just arrived from the States, looked in her turn from father to daughter, and smiled. Being a woman of the world, and endowed with strong sympathies, she saw at once that there had been love-making between these two. She held out her hand to Annette.

"Glad we have permission. We will go this afternoon—if you have no engagement." Annette merely pressed the extended hand and murmured acquiescence, for she too had received a revelation.

Gossip had suggested that Sir John was in- fatuated with this American widow. It must be so, or he would never have given this unexpected nermission, she thought. _ .

Within iwo hours 'f the short conversation— pregnant'as it was with interest—the motor-car stopped at Harold Fyfe's studio door. As if an electric light had been suddenly turned on, his whole being became illumined when he saw Annette, his "pale, rare" Annette, for white as jasmine had she become during the last few weeks.

He had not, however, time to speak of his con- tentment and joy, in once more beholding the vision for which he had so longed and prayed. An even greater surprise than the coming of Annette awaited him, and he burled forth an exclamation that was so tempestuous as to make Mrs. SmallwOod, who was on the listen and much excited by the arrival of the well-known motor-car, think her young gentleman had suddenly gone mad from unexpected pleasure. Yet it was not Annette who had called forth this outburst of excitement, but her companion. "Mrs. La Porte!" was Barold Fyfe's exclama- tion, when he had sufficiently recovered from his astonishment to speak at all. She, meanwhile, took no notice of him, but, rushing forwai'd to the corner of the studio where curly-headed Jo was playing with some toys, caught the bewildered child up in her arms and sobbed forth between kisses:

"My Josselin—my darling—my baby beauty ! " The long-lost mother had come at last, verify- ing once more the old proverb that nothing ever '2appens but the unexpected, for that she and Annette should suddenly appear together was the last occurrence Harold had ever dreamt of. Even his rapture at once more beholdhig his lost love took second place, so overwhelmed was he by Mrs. La Porte having come to claim the boy after these long months and years of absence.

Where had she been? Why had she not come before or sent supplies? were the natural ques- tions that followed her-descent on the studio. It ' was Annette who put them. Harold was far too dazed to be practieal. The explanation given in a few words was painful though delicately put—for a true woman as Marie La Porte was, is ever anxious to shield even the worst and most degraded of husbands. Her listeners could not, however, help gathering how much she had suffered during her short married life' end what a release fnieedom from the tie must have brought. "Mr. La Forte," she told them, "had got into difficulties and made her leave the child with Mrs. Fyfe, since it was necessary for him to return to America. He was an unscrupulous man and would not have paid a cent for the boy if he could have got anyone to keep him for nothing. The year's payment in advance that Mrs. Fyfe had received had, unknown to her lord and master, come from the wretched, heartbroken mother. Since that payment till Mr. La Porte died she had had no money to send, and then i'rom the old address her letters were returned. Mrs. Fyfe was dead and Harold had removed to Mitcham-street. At last, settling her affairs in New York so as to give her a small income, she started for Europe in search of her baby Josselin, and here he was the sweetest, best-cared-for little darling in all the land." Back to Lowndes-street they bore him—even presuming to make Harold accompany them thither. Mrs. La Porte would take no denial, when the young fellow urged that his presence would be unwelcome to Sir John.

"Leave her boy's elder brother out of the rejoicing that he had been found—no, that she would not ! "

Probably Mrs. La Porte was fully aware that a citadel is best carried by storm. And, clever, loving woman that she was, she carried it. Sir John gave in. • How could he do otherwise when he himself had been shot by Cupid's arrow and lokked for- ward to making Marie La Porte forget the wretchedness and suspense of the last few years. As Annette—her pretty head on Harold's shoulder when they stood together in the studio a few days later—said, smiling up into his now happy face: "If it hadn't been for little Jo, where would our happiness have been? You know the old saying, Harold, about entertaining angels unawares."

'I know I am entertaining an angel now," he answered, kissing her and laughing. She moved away, pretending to be indignant, and threatened to call Mrs. Smallwood as a chaperone. Not that that devoted old body would have been of the slightest use. She was far too much delighted that the young people should once more be together to make the smallest effort to separate them. At that very moment ,she was downstairs chattering and giving her sentiments to an old acquaintance. "Well, I never did think in all my life I could feel so jolly, and it ain't stimulants nor no artificial help as has done it—just real pleasure about them two young ones, and to think as our Jo belongs to the family after all; and if I don't dance at the weddings—well, my name ain't Sarah Smallwood! To see Jo in his page's dress —the beauty that he is—would alone make an old girl young again. And then, would you believe It they is goin' to keep me on as housekeeper! Vear heart, Sarah Smallwood has wandered into real good luck, that she has, and she's grateful, mind you—no more grumbles!" [THE END.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

SOME PRETTY GOWNS.

Lingerie gowns are being much made en prin.- nesse with rows of horizontal insertion from the hem to the shoulder, giving great slenderness to the waist. Never were •there more white mus- Ens made; the question is, says the Queen, when we shall wear them. Chiffon dinner gowns are often made with open jackets, and shaped vests beneath the basque cut in many battlements, the skirt with a couple of. flounces embroidered in festoons of flowers of the most delicate hue. Tender pink crepe-de-chine, with cross-cut deep tlounces more like tucks ,than flounces, are quite charming, especially when made with the cape sleeves, and small boleros ending well above the waistband, double-breasted and bordered with rich embroidery. Cloths are worn in tender colours for summer day wear and evening, and soft glace silk. Taffetas chiffon makes a delight- ful gown, and one that will last through to the autumn. Nothing is a better standby in the wardrobe. Baby bodices are coming to the front again, due doubtless to the prevalence of the Empire short waist.

The pretty hat shewn with this dress is of white embroidery, and is trimmed with a long, flowing scarf of red- and-white chiffon. ABOUT BATHIN-G-DIIESSES.

The practical swimmer has long since come to the conclusion, says Mrs. Jack May in the July World and His Wife. that no type of bathing. dress comes up to the simply planned affair of navy serge or bunting, the. kniOkers terminating well above the knees, a position also followed by the short, full skirt. She is also fully determined to dispense with shoes, stockings, and anything in the shape of ornamental headgear, preferring rather the utilitarian, all-enveloping rubber cap. The bather of more dilettante.description, how- ever, is permitted a far greater range for her inventive imagination, several materials, includ- ing washing satin, being requisitioned to the fabricating cause. Linen intt some pretty, artis- tic shade fashions the daintiest dresses, and calls for endless individual efforts in the matter of decoration. A biscuit-coloured linen embroi- dered in bright red poppies offers itself as origi- nal and decorative, white linen worked with • pale-pink dog-roses suggesting a delightfully cool-looking scheme that calls for the best ap- preciation. With either of these silk handker- chief caps could be worn, these naturally re- peating the colour of the embroidery, and it is : quite correct to complete the attire with stock- itig7-, and sandal shoes. Be the fabricating medium what it may, however, a fundamental point to be borne seriously in mind by those , wishing to present a smart, trim aspect is that the gown must be well cut and well made, both details that ensure it looking as well after ini- meryion as before. And another detail on no account to be overlooked is the peignoir, most of the representative bathing resorts electing to follow the French fashion of stationary 'tents. that frequently necessitate a long walk to and from tho water.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

THE ELECTRIC SLEEP.

Electric sleep, or anesthesia, is. produced by the action on the . brain of intermittent electric curronte of low voltage. It has been a subject of special study . for several years by Professor . Stephane Leduc, f Nantes, and others, and the application has been perfected until it is practicable to put dogs and rabbits quickly intd a calm and regular sleep, with general and complete anesthesia. In rabbits, at least, the sleep can be safely prolonged to eight hours or more, although ether . and chloroform bring much risk after two hours.

===== 0002601_19070713_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
EYES AND THE ELECTRIC LIGHT

A special inflammation of • the eyes— ophthalmia electrica—seems to have become common among ivorkers with electric light apparatus, and now Dr. Cnellitzer has pointed out to the Berlin Medical Society that the trouble may result from very brief exposure to strong glare. In an electric melt- ing of iron at an ,engine factory an enormous arc ,of 50,000 candle-power was produced. Workmen passing within six or eight yards were warned not to linger, yet on the next day twelve of the men sought medical advice, reporting pain in the eyes, profuse tears, spasm of the eyelids, and headache. Ultra- violet rays are the suggested active cause.

===== 0002601_19070713_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
WOOD AS STRONG AS IRON

Official tests of the many valuable hard- woods native to Western Australia have made known the extraordinary properties of yate, believed to be the strongest of all known woods. Its average tensile strength is 24,0001 b. to the square inch, equalling that of good east 'iron. But many specimens are much stronger, and one was tested up to 171 tons to-the- square inch, which is equal to the tensile strength of wrought iron. The sawn timber of yate is •probably the strongest in the world. The tree grows to a maximum of 100 ft., and has sometimes a diameter of 21ft. or, even 3ft.

A NEW POLE STAR.

The constellation of Lyra is specially in- teresting to us. says the Westminster Gazette, for towards it the sun and its planets, in eluding the earth, are flying through space at a speed of 750 Miles a minute. The dis- covery of the motion of the sun and of its direction was due to Sir William Herschel, and, as Sir Robert Ball says, though mathe- maticsans have since exhausted every refine- ment of their science in dealing with this fascinating problem, they have only "con- firmed the truth of that splendid theory," Lyra, too, is destined one day to provide our suc-cessors with a new Pole Star, as one re- sult of the phenomenon of the procession of the Equinoxes is to cause the 'earth's axis to change its point of direction, with reference to the stars, and Vega will eventually become a far more brilliant Pole Star than our old friend Polaris in Ursa Minor.

I UTILISING WASTE INDIARUBBER. ! In a paper published in the Journal of the Society of Chemical Industry, Mr. V 4 F. Reid refers to a process devised for the re- generation of vulcanised indiarubber waste. This process is based on an observation made in 1906 by Tixier, a French chemist—namely; that vulcanised rubber is completely soluble in terpineol. The rubber waste, whether vul- i canised or not, is reduced , to pulp and digested at 100 deg. to 150 deg. C. with twice ; its weight of this solvent, the operation , being performed •in a closed vessel fitted with a mechanical stirrer. The solution thus ob- tained is agitated with four times its volume of benzine, which precipitates certain im- • purities. The clear liquid is then decanted, the benzine distilled off, and the rubber pre- cipitated in the residual solution by the ad- dition of alcohol or acetone. When .prepared in this way i regenerated rubber resembles the natural product very closely; it is very vis- cid, and can again be vulcanised. It resists chemical reagents to a very considerable ex- tent, probably owing to the fact that the method of :preparation eliminates the resin- , ous impurities originally present in the rubber, which are most readily attacked by acids or alkalis. As the cost of the terpineol required in this process is by no means pro- hibitive, and the , solvent itself can be pur- chased in fairly large quantities, the method is one capable of very wide application.

THE TEMPERATURE OF MARS.

Hitherto the chief obstacle to the belief that Mars is habitable by any such beings as inhabit the earth has been the extremely low temperature probably * obtaining on the Mar- tian surface, but in No. 25, vol. xhi. of the Proceedings of the American Academy of Arts and Sciences, Professor Lowell shews that, by taking all the phenomena into con- sideration, this obstacle may be removed. Previous calculations of the temperature have beea *deduced solely from the relative dis- tance of Mars front the sun, and a recent in 7 vestigation gave —33 deg. F. as the mean temperature of the planet. Professor Lowell points out, however, that other factors, such as the relative albedoes of the planets, the screening effect of clouds, the blanketing effect of the atmosphere, &c., should be taken into account, and, on this basis, he finds that the mean annual temperature of Mars, if the heat were retained as well there as here, would be about 72deg. F. As the 're- tention is greater in the case of the earth, this value is considerably reduced in the final calculation, taking all the known factors into consideration, and a mean temperature of about 47deg. F. is ' obtained. Professor Lowell, alas finds that the boiling point of water on Mars would be about 111 deg. F. (44deg. C.), that the amount of air per unit surface is about two-ninths that found in the case, of the earth, while the relative density of the air at the surface is only about one-twelfth.

. • A TANTALISING METAL. • A rare metal which has attracted univer'sal attention during- the , last few years, while before it was scarcely known outside of some scientific laboratories,. is tantalum, so named by its discoverer. Ekelberg, on account of the tantalising difficulties experienced in ex- tracting the metal from its ores. Only a few years ago was it found out by German scien- tists, says the Enginering ilfirgathie, that I what the tfikoverer though, t to be tantalum was an alloy of it and a carbide, and .more tantalising effOrts were. necessary to produce this metal in its perfectly pure state ; but this success once being reached, the metal immediately became of such importance that it is now revolutionising at least one large industry, that of the manufacture of incan- descent lamps for electric lights. Although it has been tried to t- use tantalum as a steel- hardening agent, the # mechanical difficulties in 'alloying tantalum with steel have been s o great ,that we shall probably see other appli- cations come into prominence first. ! Tantalum is *one of the hardest substances known, and its use for scientific instruments, where price •stands always second to ac- curacy and durability, will doubtless be de- veloped much sooner, inasmuch as tantalum also resists the corrosive . influence of acids and alkaline solutions in a very remarkable degree. One of the most striking experiments ' to demonstrate the hardness of tantalum was that for the purpose, or boring a hole through sheet of pure tantalum metal a diamond kill-bore was used, revolving incessantly for, three days and making 5,000 revolutions Der ' minute, and the result was a aepression of joss than one one-hundredth of an inch in the metal, but .An entirely dull and smooth liamond.

THE " SALE " VOCABULARY'.

Have you noticed, says "Lady Phyllis" in the Bystander, that sales claim quite a vocabulary of their own? There are "Genuine Sales" (and the opposite, of which we will not speak), " Sen- t,ational Sales," "Exceptional Bargains." "Total Clearances," and disposals of "Mr. Somebody's Entire Stock." There are also "Shillin Sales." I do not deny that the bargains at theg last are wonderful, and if you are very strong and deter- mined you may stand them, though not exactly with comfort at least by endurance. But people have been known to come away from Shilling Sales requiring the dressmaker to renovate their crushed or torn clothes and the doctor to patch up their nerves. So they are not cheap in the end. r, TO-DAY'S RECIPE. tdROTTTES D'ASPERGES AUX CEErFS POCRES.—

Prepare a bundle of asparagus for cooking, boil in the ordinary way until tender, and then drain. In the meantime put Noz. of butter into a saucepan, and when it has melted stir in a flat tablespoonful of flour—stirring it continu- ously—and add by degrees a gill of milk, flavoured with lemon, and seasoned with salt, cayenne, and a very little French mustard. When the sauce has thickened, stir into it two tabirspoo.ututs ot rich cream and a ciessert- spoonful of tomato sauce. Serve the asparagus on slices of buttered toast, dress the top with aa many poached eggs ail are required, ppur tato sauce oyer, 80 serve.

Every doctor has his favourite prescription which he recommends in case of the system being generally run down, the symptoms of which are usually pains in the back, headache, constipation, and where the patient is subject to it—rheumatism. 'The following recipe is the favourite of an eminent physician :—Tinctnre of Buchu one ounce; Karox Compound,one ounce; Syrup .of Lemon,two ounces, and has been remarkably successful in its healing and sooth- ing effects upon the entire kidney and Urinary structure often overcoming the worse forms of Backache and Bladder weakness in just a little while. The ingredients are harmless and can easily be mixed by shaking well in a bottle, or the prescription can be made up by any local chemist at a small cost. Take one teaspoonful after each meal and at bed-time. Try it for a week and note result,

Distance seems to lend enchantment to the seaside resort, as far as the day tripper is concerned, and the result is that he is apt to overlook the charms of places almost at his doors. ' The employees of Messrs Peter Walker and Sim, Limited, have journeyed far and wide for many of their, annual outings, but this year they had a change and kept within the borders of the county palatine. On Saturday there was an invasion of Southport by the brewery workpeople and their families, between 1,400 and 1,500 people availing themselves of the generosity of the firm who have for many years given their employees this much-appre- ciated opportunity for enjoyment amid fresh scenes. The fact that Southport is so easy of access proved rather an advantage than otherwise, and the trip was voted one of the most successful and enjoyable on record Sotnething must be said of the weather, although the theme has been well nigh exhausted of late. Saturday would have passed muster tolerably well as an early spring day. There was nothing typical of July—no scorching sunshine or gentle zephyrs,—but recent experience has taught us.to be thankful for small mercies, and the trippers rejoiced in the fact that there was hardly any rain to interfere with the day's pleasure. The air was quite fresh , and invigorating, and the only drawback was that towards evening it became very chilly. One great advantage of visiting a town so near as qouthport was that the excursionists were not called upon to break their repose at an unearthly hour and rush to the station, for the earliest train did not leave until eight o'clock. By that time Central Station was thronged with a merry multitude who never for a moment questioned that the day would be fine, although most of them were armed with umbrellas or overcoats. The task of transporting the crowd to the seaside was admirably fulfilled by the Cheshire Lines Committee, the trains being got off promptly to time and accompliffhing the run in a little over an hour. The morning was beautifully clear and the picturesque country in the neighbourhood of Gateacre was looking at its best. At Birkdale a contingent of lads from Manchester who are enjoying their summer encampment ceased their games for a moment to cheer lustily, and although there has been so little warm sunshine the youngsters .were looking bronzed and the very pictures of health. The trippers were in high spirits when Southport was reached, and pro- ceeded to make the best of the long day at their dis- posal. So much has been written of Southport and its manifold attractions that little need be said here. Without exaggeration, Southport may be described as a model town, for its fine boulevards, charm- ing in their wealth of foliage, give it an r of disti u ction quite unique. It is a pity that the sea keeps Southport at such a respectful distance. The intending visitor is gener- ally asked by solicitous friends if he has provided himself with a telescope and is told that he will certainly need it if he wishes to enjoy the luxury of al sea view. However, oft Saturday the water was quite I visible to the naked eye, and the foard-capped waves were a pleasing sight to the inland dweller. If the sea so unkindly avoids Southp)rt, Southport has made every advance towards the sea and there is a fine, long pier by which one can get right out to the briny. , Messrs. Walker's excursionists had free admission to this grand promenade and ,nothing could be more exhilarating than a stroll to the far end. Those who had imagined that Blackpool possesses a mmopoly of breezes were agreeablY surprised, for on the pier the salt-laden wind simply tore round one, and quickly freed the visitor of any symptoms of inertia. "Blackpool's a fool to it " declared one tripper to her companion as they struggled bravely along, pausing often to take breath. The other would lik to have given up the effort, but her enthusiastic friend waived aside objections with the verdict that "they, must go ,to the end if it ' snuffed ' them!" And to the end they went! Fashionable resort as Southport is, it has realized the wisdom of catering for the tripper land on the sands there are amusements galore. These the Warrington visitors were able to sample at reduced rates. They indulged in the thrilling sensations of the water-chute, flying machine and ferial flight and made the novel if not very dignified descent of the helter-skelter lighthouse. They watched the old, old escapades of Punch and Judy with a 3 much gleeful interest as if the show had been one, of the most up-to-date inventions, and patiently submitted to the jokes of the übiquitous pie rots. Some of the trippers allowed themselves to be sketched in public and paid for the privilege, whilst others had their" bumps" felt for the edification of an admiring crowd. Free trips on the Marine Lake helped the Warringtonians to ..ealize something of the joys of the sea-excursion without its unpleasant accompaniments, and the electric launches were crowded with passengers. For the musically-inclined, the Corporation band in Lord-street provided a spendid entertainment araid lovely surroundings: In the evening,when innumerable coloured lights sparkled among the trees, the scene could be Jikened to a fairy- land. The long day with all its pleasures, like every- thing else, came to an end and whefl train time arrived the trippers, tired with the exertion of amusing themselves, were quite ready for home. The first train took its departure at 90 and the others followtd at 9.40, and 10 o'olock, all reaching the good old town " without mishap after one of the most successful excursions in the annils of the firm.. A word of praise is due to Councillor Fulton Smith, upon whom the arrangements for the trip largely devolved, and who did his utmost to ensure a happy day for the workpeoPle: He must have been amply rewarded by knowing that not a single untoward incident marred the success of the outing.

THE SANIOUR.

A stranger in a world His own The Saviour spent His days; And wondrous was the love that shone In all His works and ways. Heir to an empire yet to come, A lowly life He led ; Fr earth had not for Him a home, Nor place to lay His head. A lonely Man, in humble guise, He went from place to place ; And men beheld, with wondering eyes, His works of love and &ace. In crowded town, on mountain steep, On shore, and lonely sea; He sought the outcast, wandering sheep With. His salvation free. And wheresoever He was found, Where'er His voice was heard; The poor and needy gathered round And all His pity shared. The weary souls that longed for rest, Sad eyes with sorrow dim ; And men with want and Pain oppressed, All found relief in Him. For these His journeys all were made, To them His love drew nigh ; And,blessed thought, for such He prayed When stars were in the sky. But all, the men who loved their dross, Had nought for Him but scorn ; The Throne they gave Him was a Cross, With Crown of piercing thorn. And so it is alas, to-day, The world knows not the Lord; For pride and riches bar the way Against His living Word. But now, as in the days of old, The poor still hear His voice ; ' Still gather to His blessed fold, And in His love rejoice. J. H. W. CLINDINNING

July, 1907

The Examiner, Saturday, July 13, 1907.

anyone engaged in the drink traffic was the few who ; abused it,—not those who were &An to control themselves and who were encouraged to do so udder such ' a firm as Peter Walker and Son. Under the management system there was no temptation of any kind to go in for i refreshment at any of their houses beyond what a person required. Each manager was forbidden at the peril of his situation to sit down and drink with any customer. No barmaids were employed by Messrs. Walker and not even an innocent piano was permitted within the precints of their establishments. If there wasany inducement for a man to go over the score it was certainly not in Niessrs. Walker's houses. (Applause.) He believed that when customers entered their houses in the morning before beginning their day's work— especially in the case of the dock labourers—they found on the table solid refrPshment before-they were allowed to take any liquor at all. If that was not one of the best helps to temperance he did not -know what it was, because temperance meaut that a man should control himself and have the privilege of feeling that when he entered such estab- lishments as belonged to Messrs. Walker he was not bound to drink any intoxicating liquor, but could have tea, coffee, or any of the innocent refreshments which teetotallers professed to believe so much in. lie did not know so much about. teetotallers, because he hit.l never been one. (Laughter.) But so far as he could read, the teetotaller was a man who had a one-sided view of life. He did not seem to give consideration to his neighbour at all, and he believed that because a man took a class of wine or a glass,of beer when he felt he wanted it, he was gorlig to perdition. (Laughter.) lie (the speaker) did not know that he had, gone to perdition yet. (Laughter). He found that becaese he was not restrained altogether from taking what he chose to take without let or hind- rance, he did not go over the score. lie believed if he were prohibited from taking beer in the brewery he would go over the score from sheer perversity of temper. (Laughter.) He had Apostolic authority for what he had said, because St. Paul said that until the law came he did not not know what, sin was. If they told him (the speaker) that it was wrong for him to drink he would be sure to go wrong, so he hoped they would not follow his example. (Laughter.) Again thanking the Company on behalf of the firm, Councillor Fulton Smith said he was associated with the present managing directors when they were learning business in Scotland. They were still carrying out the same rigid principles then instilled into thorn of putting business before pleasure and seeing that everyone interested in* the concern did his part to promote its progress. He hored they. would have many more happy gatherings , like the one they had had the pleasure of .enjoying that , day. (Applause.) The Chairman announced that Mr. Mouro was pre- vented from attending owing to a family bereavement and wired expressing his regret.Otherapolcories had been I received from Mr. J. Lyon Whittle and MI. Carson, of Liverpool. The toast of" The Railway Companies and Repre- sentatives" was honoured on the proposition of the Chairman, and Mr. Townley (L. & N.-W.) responded. Captain Potter proposed the health of "The Visitors." Mr. Upson responded and paid a tribute to Councillor Fulton-Smith's public work, especially as Chairman of the Education Committee. He (Mr. (Jpson) was pleased to accept Councillor Fulton-Smith's invitation to join that gathering because it brought him into a new atmos- phere. He•bad had the pleasure of making friends that day, and he had proved too that some of the employees of the firm, at any rate, would bring honour to any firm. (Applause.) It was a delight on an occasion like that to find that the many differences that so frequently divided them were banished and they could enjoy hearty fellowship together. (Applause). Mr. E. G. Donn elly (" Southport Visiter ") and Dr. Bmide also responded. The health of the Chairman was drunk on the proposi- tion of Mr. Parry and the proceedings closed with the National Anthem.

messrs. Walker's Brewery Excursion.

SPEECH-MAKING AT THE DINNER. COUNCILLOR FULTON-SMITH AND TEETOTALLERS.

In the evening the staff and a number of guests were entertained at dinner at the Prince of Wales Hotel. Upson,renresenta- tives of the railway companies and others. An escellent repast having been served, the loyal toasts were honoured on the proposition of the Chairman. Alderman Francomb proposed the toast of the evening, "Success to the firm of Peter Walker and 'Son, Ltd." He said the firm was One of the most successful in the country—(hear, hear)--rind he believed to-day it, was, as successful, or Ihren more so, than it had ever been before. (Applause). There were very few firms conducted on better lines, and he was certain that ?veryone connectedjwith the concern did his best, to ensure its prospbrity. They deeply regretted the absence of the very able manager, Mr. Monro, who had always delighted to take an interest in those on ting.,and they hope,i he would he present, with them on many future occasions. (Applanse.) The firm seemed to have been blessed for many years-with eNeel- lent servants, from the manager downwards. They were excellent master,, end he was sure all the visitors appre- ciated the splendid treat they had given that day. (Apnlensel. . Mr. John Lee seconded, and endorsed all that Alder- man Francomb had' said. He had received nothing but the greatest court.sy from Councillor Fulton Smith, end he regh.rded the firm as the finest in the world. (Applause). The toast was heartily honoured. _ _

If the firm prospered "hen the ' wages WOOld go on, as they had done for many years, ..y;thoet interruption. Ibecause human nature would exist so long as there were human beings on the face of the earth. So long as) desire was in the human heart, that, desire must be I gratified. HOUSE COMMITTEE. At a meeting of this committee Mrs. Carruthers was appointed a member of the Ladies' Visiting Committee in place of Mrs. J. Lyon Whittle, resigned. The MAster submitted the replies received from the master builders re Thomas Hinde and it was resolved that the same lie on the table.

[The minutes were approved on the proposition of Mr. Billinge seconded by Mr.Vather. THE CHAPLAIN'S SALARY.

The Local Government Board wrote consenting to the increase of the salary of the Rev. W. Bracecamp as chaplain from £5O to £75. [he CLERK also read the following resolution which was passed at the meeting of the Independent Labour Party held on Monday last at Friars Green house :—" That this meeting protests against the action of the War-, rington Board of Guardians in increasing the salary of this Rev. W. Bracecamp." .

There was no comment on the matter. s INCREASED CONTRIBUTIQN.

A letter was read from the secretary to the Blhad Children's Home, Princes Park, Liverpool, stating that he was directed by the managers to acquaint the Board with the following facts :—That the expenses of carry- ing on the institution so much exceed the income that it has become utterly impossible to carry on the sch'ool any longer unless an increased rate is paid. That it has been and still is the cheapest school of its kind in the kingdom and thoroughly efficient. That the Liverpool Education Committee have made a thorough investiga- tion of the question and have willingly agreed to pay the increased rate, and that this increase has been sanctioned by the Board of F.ciocation. . _

The Board decided- Co pay, an increased annual con- tribution of £2l' per child maintained in the home, as against the present rate of £l6: WORKMEN'S CpMPENSA VON ArT. This was approved.

Warrington second also had -the satisfactiOn of scoring their record total for the season in .202 for 5 wickets, on the StanloY ground. The home team had first knock and Bennett and Hawtin proceeded to reap a rich harvest in wickets the former taking five for five and the latter two for twelve, with the result that the Stanley total reached only 35. As was the case with the first team Warrington started badly when Thompson and Houghton proceeded to place the issue beyond doubt. Both treated the bowling with scant courtesy and the century was hoisted after less than an hour's play. 140 was run up before Thompson left after having made 83 and twelve runs later Houghton was also beaten when eight past the half century. Pierpoint and Dolan followed the example and when the score was 202 and the innings was declared closed were both not out with 21 ruus each.

Stanley occupy 1)t a lowly position in the League table, and judging by their performance at Warrington on Saturday they are not likely to irunroye their pros- pects. They batted first on a damp wicket and were all out for 29 in less than an hour. Not a single member of the ter.m could time thedlliyeries of Cresswell and Callon and the highest individual score for the side was 6by S nythe. Callon came oat with thesplendid record of 6 wickets for two runs each, whilst Cresswell claimed three victims for 13 runs. The two bowlers have so far done all the attacking of the season and have met with remarkable success as the appendei details disclose : runs per wkt. 431 5'25

Warrington made a disastrous start, as with one run on the sheet Alsnp held a hard eturn from Callon. Two runs later Connor fell a victim to smart fielding, Stott° bringing off a clover catch. Podmore and Wright. became associated and both played confidently. Podmore opened his account by two boundaries off. 'lloore, and Wright displaying the same proclivity for hard hitting, the spectators were treated to a delightful exhibition. A change in the bowling resulted in the rate of scoring being increased. The fifty was hoisted, brit ten runs later Podmnre retired as the result of an indifferent stroke. Wright was also badly missed. The ball bounced badly, and catching the shoulder of the bat glanced into the slip—the easieFt possible of chances. So siire was Wright that he was out that he was proceeding to the pavilion when the catch was missed. He signalized his escape by a couple of fine drives which called for cheers from the spectators.

Silcock was hardly so aggressive' but several loose balls were banged to the boundary by him and eighty was passed when Wright was clean bowled by Alsop. the ball breaking in and taking his -leg stump. He had credited himself with 49 and Stanley were doubtless glad to see the last of him. His place was however taken by another hitter in the person of Bibby and Stanley continued to indulge in vigorous leather hunt- ing. The captain quickly passed Silcock's score but. in the opinion of the spectators, both Men played more carefully than the state of ,the game needed and their efforts would have been more popnlar had they hit out a little more freely and taken more risks. Silcock was particularly careful. He is-a first-class bat and offered a sound defence with the result that he scored slowly. Seventy-four runs were added when Bibby was held by the bowler with 48 to his credit. Silcock continued to play sound if slow cricket and he saw Richardson add 20 to the score and r6turn to the pavilion and assisted in the bGisting of the second century before Moore beat him with a good-ball. The remaining men offered little resistance and the innings closed for 224 —Warrington's highest aggregate for the season.

Warrington Police team on Thum(lay journeyed to Southport and snatched a vicbory from the preservers of the peace of that town. The teams were evenly matched and in the end the Warrington men won by 13. They were afterwards entertained to tea.

* Signifies not out.

Swimming

Warrington polo team accomplished a smart perform- ance on Thursday evening by defeating Everton. The match was in the first division of the Liverpool and District League and the local team had a defeat to wipe off having lost by 2-1 with Everton earlier in the season. There was a crowded attendance. Everton in the first half went away with a rush hut were well kept out. Even play followed when Warrington's first goal was scored under unusual circumstances. The visitors were attacking hotly when C. Ashton received the ball in the Warrington goal mouth and with a beautifully long shot defeated Morris at. the other end of the bath.

Skinner tried hard to equalise and Woods was severely tested. C. Ashton however again came to the rescue and dribbling down the bath, ended with again scoring. The second half was even more vigorously fought but neither side could score and Warrington were returned winners by two goals to nil.

C. Ashton was the most prominent player in the bath, and was a constant source of anxiety to the Everton defence,his long and fast shots coming no little trouble. Jones for the visitors played a splendid game at full back and was largely instrumental in keeping down the score. A word of praise must be uttered of the work of both goal keepers. Time after time they were called upon to deal with tricky shots and both brought off some remarkably fine saves.

"The teams meet again next Tuesday in the second round of the Northern Counties Cup Competi- ion, the match this time being at Everton.

Prior to Thursday's fixture a novel feature was intro- duced in the shape of a twenty-five yards race for those schoolboys who have been taught to swim this season by Professor Barlow. Some eighty boys c?rnpeted,and the fact that so many have learnt how to keep them- selves above water more than justified any expense to which the town has been put by their education. Many displayed great promise, and by judicious handicapping the final produced a close finisb, the winners being. W. Leah, 1 ; A.A. Iddon, 2; D.Vernon, 3 ; and J. Whitfield, 4.
