THE EX ,A

JULK 13th. 1907

THE visit of the King and THE KING AND Queen to Ireland comes at IRELAND. the right time. Matters on

the Other side of thel -Channel have not been going particularly well of late. Agrarian trouble has once again shown its I sinister form in the West, and there is little doubt that agitation, partly political and partly local, I threatens to extend and iniensify in the near future. Irish affairs srp not, perhaps, easy for the :Englishman to understand. He knows that in the past many sincere efforts have been made to settle the eternal land question. The British tax- I Payer has been asked time and again to clontribute for the relief of the distressed Irish. :Four years ago his contribution was to less than £12,000,000 under the Land purchase Act. It is not encouraging to him to be told that that sacrifice has not succeeded in pro- ducing the settlement of the land question for Which he hoped. Twice lately the House of Commons has considered the position in Ireland with regard to land purchase. On the first occa- sion the complaint from across the Channel was that in consequence of losses on the flotation of the Loan under the Land Act of 1903 the machinery of that Act had practically broken down, and that unless some relief were afforded from the British Exchequer the whole of the burden would fall upon the Irish ratepayer, who, owing to the impoverishment of the country, Would be unable to support it, and the local -government of the country would be brought to a standstill. The second debate was that dealing With the Evicted Tenants Bill, brought in by th Government. That Bill proposes to reinstate on small farms "the wounded soldiers of the Land War." Objection is taken by the Opposition because the Government purpose to acquire the land necessary by compulsory methods instead of by the voluntary arrangement by means of which land is acquired under the Land Purchase Act. It is contended that this will mean considerable hardship to landholders who may be compelled to tart with land against the management or use of which no complaint can be made, and who may tot even have ever had occasion to evict any of heir tenents.

These several questions, waiting as they are for Battlement, must necessarily affect largely the political atmosphere in Ireland at the present time. But however strongly they may feel upon matters of government Irish people, of whatever complexion politically they may be, have united in offering to the KING and QUEEN a sincere and hearty welcome to the island. Much talk is made, when political matters are under discussion, of the supposed disloyalty of a certain section of Irish- men to the British Crown. Whatever may have been the case in the past—and there have, of course, been many Irishmen who regarded the proper settlement of the Irish question as one in which separation from the British Crown was essential—such ambitions do not at the present moment Claim many adherents in Ireland. What- ever the demand for separate Government, dis- loyalty to the British Crown is not part of the national feeling. And a great deal of the change is due to the present King. His Majesty possesses one characteristic at least that appeals especially to Irish men and women. He is a great sports- man, and that goes a long way with his Gaelic; subjects. Their welcome to the Royal visitors has proved that in personal attachment to the Crown Dublin and Kingstown are, at any rate,not behind the other leading cities of the kingdom. That His Majesty should have selected the present moment to pay his visit is one more proof of his tact. Ireland had hoped for something politically palliative in the Irish Bill which the Government had promised in the King's Speech. That hope, for reasons which it is not necessary to discuss, has been disappointed for the present Parliament. But although the main Irish question is still unsettled, King Edward has given evi- dence, by his journey across the Irish Channel, that he intends to allow no oPportunity to pass of showing how strongly he regards the connection between the British Crown and the Irish people. Incidentally the visit has been marked by a rather unfortunate Incident in the theft of 250,000 worth of jewels of the Insignia of the Order of St. Patrick. Dublin

Castle, which is the cockshy of nearly all the political factions in Ireland, comes rather badly out of the affair. Indeed, the unfortunate Castle is being unmercifully chaffed for its " incompe- tence" in not being able to guard the treasures within its walls. The disappearance of the insignia, though it has proved inconvenient, has not interfered with the success of the KING'S I visit, which is certain to be fraught with moll out results in convincing the Irish people that they have the sincere affection and sympathy of the Crown.

The charge is sometiples FREEDOM FOR made against the Govern- TEACHERS. ment that it has done nothing to remove the educational disabilities under which Noncon- formists labour, but at last the reproach—never a very fair one—is removed. By a stroke of the pen, religious tests in training colleges have been abolished, and these valuable institutions thrown open to all and sundry without let or hindrance other than the limitation of seats and stone walls. The "Regulations for the Training of the Teachers and the Examination in Training Colleges" which were issued by the Board of Education on Wednesday contain one of the most important and far-reaching reforms which the present Government has yet attempted. The great majority of places in training colleges are in practice reserved for the Church of England. The Church has built most of the colleges, but it is the State which pays most of the cost of their maintenance as educational institutions. In many cases the Church contribution is infinitesimal; yet admission to the colleges is reserved exclu- sively for Churchmen. This is the state of things which the new regulations should effectively remedy.

Among the new regulations which have been included in the conditions governing the Parlia- mentary grant is one which prescribes that

In no circumstances may the application of a candidate be rejected on the ground of religious faith or by reason of his refusal to undertake to attend or abstain from attending any place of religious worship or any religious observance or instruction in religious subjects in the college or elsewhere; nor on the ground of social antece- dents or the like. It is indeed amazing that denominational tests which disappeared from the universities more than a generation ago should have survived in the training colleges until now, and the gratification will be great all over the country that this grave injustice has been at last removed. Elaborate rules are made with a view to the enforce- ment of the charter of freedom, and any College or Hostel infringing it is to be fined for a first offence and to have its grants withdrawn altogether on a second occasion. There might be some difficulty in enforcing the regulation if there were bad faith on the part of the authori- ties of the college. The demand for places is often in excess of the supply, and it would be easy to confuse the grounds on which priority was given to particular candidates. But the regulations are so detailed, and the penalties so severe, as to counteract any temptation, should it ever exist (and we hope that it seldom would), to bad faith.

In the case of new colleges, the undenomina tional principle is for the first time fully estab- lished. After the Ist of August next no institu- tion not already recognised as a Training College or Hostel will be so recognised unless it is undenominational. To obtain recognition, it must not require any teacher to belong or not to belong to any particular religious denomination. There must similarly be no religious tests in the case of the majority of the members of its governing body. And, finally, "no catechism or formulary distinctive of any particular religious denomination" must be taught except where the parent or guardian asks for it; in which case, if the College decides to supply the denominational demand, the cost is not to fall on public funds. It will be seen at once how compre- hensive these regulations are. All future Training Colleges in receipt of public grants are to be strictly undenominational. Mr. McKenna is to be congratulated on the firm and thoroughgoing way in which he has solvtid this problem and put everyone on an equal educational footing so far as the Training Colleges are concerned. It only remains now for the Government to peaceably or forcibly persuade the House of Lords into sanctioning the freeing of appointments to schools, and the bitterest of Nonconformist grievances will be things of the past,

, More is likely to be heard THE ART of the appointment of a MASTERSHIP. head-master of the School of Art. At Monday's meet- ing of the Education Committee the recom- mendation of the School Management Committee appointing Mr. C. Ma.WSOE, of Shipley, ponies up for confirmation. Since our last issue the facts relating to Mr. G. S. Pendlebury's application have become more,widely known, and there is a strong feeling that—no doubt unwittingly—the Com- mittee has done the local man an injustice. We, are assured that Mr. Pendlebury's qualifications are excellent, and at any rate they have satisfied the Board of Education, thcugh questioned by the School Management Committee. The worst feature of the incident affecting Mr. Pendlebury is that he was denied the privilege of even appearing before the Committee in company with eight other selected candidates. We should have thought this was about the minimum of consideration that a local applicant for a situation might reasonably expect. Mr. Pendlebury's reputation as an art master stands in some jeopardy, and we hope that his claim for consideration will not be casually treated by the responsible authority on Monday.

It is not often nowadays IN DEFENCE OF that one comes across a DRINK. defence of strong drink and man's right to imbibe it, but every now and then some representative of the brewing industry is constrained to take up the cudgels on behalf of that bold, bad beverage—beer, Councillor Fulton Smith, when presiding over the annual dinner given by Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons at Southport on Saturday, vigorously defended his trade. At the moment he was, largely, preaching to the converted, but as the speech was reported it acquires a wider signifi- cance. We are aware that we ought to look at Councillor Fulton Smith and his utterances through teetotal spectacles and con- demn both as equally bad, bus in common fairness we must admit that the speaker said much that was true and reasonable. We agree that it is a mistake for some people to demand that the drink-trade should be "wiped off the statute book and off the history of the country." In itself it is as honest a trade as baking bread. Properly managed, we have no fault to find with the trade as such. It supplies a legitimate demand, and a demand that will probably never cease, thitugh it may diminish. No, the brewer is not a villain merely because he is a brewer. The evils are in the methods of selling drink adopted by some brewers, and in the inordinate and unnatural thirst for stimulants of so many of our weaker brethren. Councillor Fulton

===== 0002601_19070713_art0066.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
--,• - • - - , -

===== 0002601_19070713_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, July 13, .1.907 NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Smith believed neither in the ruthless "pushing " of drink as a marketable product, nor in its reck- less consumption. His speech was largely a temperance address, for temperance in the selling and buying of drink was his chief therne."Regulate the drink" he said, "regulate the source of it and so arrange it that men will be encouraged to take care of themselves and not to abuse the privilege which drink conveys to them." This is the brewer's manager in the role of educationist and reformer. We believe in Councillor Fulton smith's sincerity and we recognise that the firm he represents is one of the best of its kind in the country. We have only one fault to find with his definition of temperance. He failed to point ou-, that steong drink is harmful and that most people would be better without it.. Doubtless, "the man who takes a glass of wine or a glass of beer when h wants it is not necessarily going to perdition." But he has got one foot on the road thither, and how far he travels rests with himself. If all men had the requisite amount of self-denial and moral force they might partake of intoxicants harmlessly, but the majority are as weak as the drink is strong. Therefore, the teetotaller is the man of wisdom, though the brewer may be unwilling to admit it. There would be no need to restrict th [sale of drink at all, if we could fortify the reason and strengthen the will of the consumer. But as long as human nature remains what it is, we shall have to handicap the brewer and preach teetotalism for the good of the race.

Mr. Oscar Asche is shortly A GOOD FIGURE to appear in a play having FOR DRSMA. the attractive title of

_ "Attila the Hun"—attractive that is to say, in a dramatic sense, for Europe has not had many more fearsome bugbears than Attilla in the fifth century. Chalons-sur-Marne, where Aetius played Wellington to his Napoleon, had, it is interesting to note, seen, two centuries before. the Emperor Aurelian defeat Tetricus, the last of the Pretenders to the throne termed the Thirty Tyrants, and on its vast camp the retreat of the French Army was announced just before "that contemptible snuff-out of Napolean the Little at Sedan " ! Attila concerns the modern reader principally because he introduces him to the fascinating theme of Europe's successive Asiatic invasions. One sees the barbarians come, wave after wave, and roll across Russia, Middle Europe, and Germany down into Spain and Italy. We had our successive settlers in England; there were, on the Continent, Saxons and Franks. Visigoths, Ostrogoths, and Longo- bardi. France, Lombardy, and Hungary, of course, derive their names from this picturesque ancestry. In those days of gloom Attila was necessarily credited with the powers of darkness (and so he had in a way). At this end of the can- tulles we did as much for Napoleon. Attila was an astute, an enterprising, and a daring warrior, , with a genius for generalship. He called himself "The Scourge of God," and he knew what he was talking about. Greek and Roman civilisation was on its trial,f or life and death,when Attila presented himself at its gates. Civilisation came well through one of its supreme ordeals, and the happy defeat of Attila made all the future possible.

Lord Selborne's Memor SOUTH AFRICAN andum on South African FEDERATION. Federation presents to the South African public per- haps the most reasoned argument in favour of a general policy of unity between the separate Colonies which lie south of Lake Tanganyika. Everything favours Federation. The war and the subsequent grant of self-government to the Transvaal and the Orange River Colonies have removed causes of friction which retarded the progress of South Africa all through the nineteenth century. For all practical pur- poses the great territory over which Lord Selbourne presides as Governor-General is now settled under the British flag, and there is no indication from any quarter that any forces are at work which will operate to disturb the position of tranquility which has been pro- duced by the trust manifested in the South Africans as a whole, irrespective of race, by the British Government and people. Now, therefore, the opportunity presents itself for South Africans to unite in putting their house in order. Federa tion is needed. One of the most fruitful sources of dispute among the different Governments in the past has been the question of railway rates, due to the fact that the railways are owned by the several Colonies, and are large sources of revenue. This has led to the adoption of a short-' sighted policy which, however it may have served to fill the coffers of the Government at a time when money was required, has been extremely bad for the prosperity and progress of Smith Africa as a whole. Again, the question of native policy can only be dealt with by the Colonies acting in con- cert and under the guiding hand of a central Government. There is, at present, great wastage of strength and opportunity in the administral- tion of internal affairs. This would unques- tionably be mitigated by a scheme of Federa- tion. If Australia and Canada needed union to enable them to realise their immense possibilities, South Africa is surely in like case. And perhaps most potent of all the influences in favour of the course which Lard Selborne advocates is the fact that the majority of statesmen in S )utla Africa itself regard the project as the ultimate object to be aimed at. The thing, of course, must come gradually from South Africa itself, but British people, to whom all that has passed is not matter for forgetfulness, will be only too pleased if this further measure of stability is taken.

* * An American tramp has broken all swimming records-27 miles in 30 minutes, The feat was accomplished under the most unfavourable circum- stances, for it was in the water-tank of a railway engine, into which he had fallen whilst trying to steal a ride. The tank was only four feet deep. but this ho did not know, and he kept swimming until the first stop. He was asked for his fare, but his reply was that he had travelled by water. o * The municipal bowling green at Victoria Park has much to thank the weather for, even if its patrons find good cause to grumble. He has a special gift for idealising ancient buildings. Mr. Fitton is locally connected also by marriage, for about twenty years ago he married the youngest sister of Mrs. Dodds of Penketh.

•* * * People who are contemplating with dismay the prospect of spending a wet holiday in fireless rooms at some partially inhabited seaside resort may be 'comforted to know (says the "Manchester Guardian ") that on Wednesday afternoon at 3.17 a new moon started on its course. The writer was confidently told by a Cheshire weather profit that the moon never changes in the afternoon withoiit producing a decided reversal in the character of the weather—from bad to good or from good to bad. The scoffer who inquires whether the last two moon changes have produced any improvement may be reminded that this is the first occasion this year on which the moon has changed in the afternoon. The previous six moons have started their career at various times between 5.' 42'9 p.m. and 8 59.3 a.m. .

* * * • The boys in a Gbildford school were told to write an essay on " Newspapers." Here are some gems; Newspapers are made of raga and grass. They are sold for different purposes. People who keep shops or sell things wrap them up in a piece of newspaper. The little fellow who wrote the following knows a thing or two: They were first called "news letters." In those days there were no pictures, no diagrams, but facts, but now they have more than facts. There are some papers which put a thing in one day and contradict it the next.

Here. are four more gems. The third looks nasty, but perhaps there is a mistake in the spelling: Some have pictures of every little thing that happens, such as two or three inches of snow in the streets of London. By means of papers grievances can be redressed, situations obtained, knowledge increased, and property regained. . There are weakly papers. You can get them for a little more than nothing. * * *

We well remember Alderman Bennett being laughed to scorn once when he pictured a municipality so well managed that instead of paying rates the inhabitants I received an annual bonus from profits. In most of the Alderman's " dreams " ,there is a substratum of I truth and reality, and this one has actually come true —though not in Warrington alas! In Germany they have recognised the principle that the land belongs to the nation and put a tax on land values and "unearned increments." No less than 1,500 towns and villages in Germany own so much common land that the inhabitants pay neither rates nor taxes, and 500 of these local 'authorities derive so great rental from their lands that they are able to pay every citizen on New Year's Day a bonus of from £5 to £2O as his share of I surplus revenue. We, in this country, pay heavy rates and allow the landlord to gobble up the land and wax fat on the development thereof without paying a penny, but the revolution of the land laws is surely coming! We recommend our readers to read every word of Mr. Ockel's interesting article headed "Germany's Example" on page 5 of this paper.

* BAND OF HOPE UNION.

There was a large attendance at a meeting at the Winmarleigh Cafe, Sankey-street, on Monday evening under the auspices of the Band of Hope Union. The Rev. J. F. Matthews presided, and the speaker was Mr. W. N. Edwards, F.C.S. London, (director of the Department of Scientific Education and Information). Those present included the Rev. J. S. Hughes, and Messrs. F.Gandy(secretary of the Warrington Union), , Weston (agent of the Lancashire and Cheshire Union), J. J. Firth (police court missionary), S. Fernley, T. Dale, and G. Whitfield. The Chairman, in the course of a short and inspiring address,commended scientific methods of teaching temperance.—Mr. Edwards said his address was for distinctly educational purposes, and he would show how to teach in their Band of Hope meetings. Such splendid work had been done in the Band of Hope cause that he would waste no words of introduction upon it, but there was no reason why they should not reach a higher altitude. They were living in an educational age, and it behoved i them to do something more n the Band of Hope meetings than they had hitherto done. They would have to try to bring their methods more in accord with elementary day school teachers'. (Hear, hear.) Unless they did that they would lose their hold on the girls and boys of the near future. When they gave a temperance or band of hope address, they must illustrate it, and he would show them some simple experiments whereby the addresses could be made more interesting. He thought the time had come when they would have to throw overboard those half-hour or three-quarters of 9n hour addresses, and introduce short but interesting ones. The lecturer then showed numerous simple chemical experiments to demonstrate the deleterious effects of alcohol.—Several uestions were asked, which the lecturer answered.— During the evening songs were given by Miss Wood- land and pianoforte and mandoline duets by Mr. B. Clatworthy and Miss Clatworthy.—Mr. F. Gandy pro- posed a vote of thanks to thq lecturer for his able address, to Mr. Matthews for presiding, to the artistes, and to Mrs. Simpson for providing hospitality for the , lecturer.—Mr. Thomas Dale seconded.—Mr. Weston put the resolution to the meeting, and it was carried.

GERMANY'S EXAMPLE. HOW THE LAND PAYS THE RATES.

The following article on "The Taxation of Land Values in Germany," by Mr. R. Ockel of Warrington, is repzinted from the current issue of " The West- minster Review " : No person visiting Germany from England can escape being struck by the great superiority in the aspect of the towns as compared with those in this country. It is not only the vastly superior paving and cleanliness of the streets, not only the well painted and often beautifully ornamented common dwelling- houses, not only the monumentally constructed and artistically adorned post offices, railway stations, museums, schools, and other public buildings; not only the broad, boulevard-like main streets, lined with rows of well-cared-for trees, and by strips of lawn with flower beds supplied the summer through with flowers in bloom • it is not only the , generously spacious crossings of such main streets, ' with their fountains, their stautes, and their park-like surroundings, that astonish the English visitor. What surprises him above all is the complete absence of slums, and of people in rags; and the merry Sundays , that the well-behaved town plpulations spend in the neighbouring pine forests, the ozone of which is a great preventive of tuberculosis; in the beer gardens, listening to high-class military concerts; or studying the museums and art galleries and thus educating themselves and raising their lives to a plane unknown in this country even amongst those enjoying double or manifold the income. What magic is it that gives German towns, even the small ones, such a palpable superiority as compared with towns of the same size in England, and German life so great a superiority as compared with English life even though German incomes are on the average so much smaller? . _

Mr. Chamberlain assures us that this magic is Pro- tection, and hence he demands what he is pleased to call " Tariff Reform !" But anyone who has read Henry George's well-known work, "Protection or Free Trade ?" or his classic " ricience of Political Economy," will at once dismiss this idea as simply absurd. The superiority noted cannot be due to, but must be in spite of Protection! Again the National Service Leaguers tells us that it is compulsory service that has accomplished this marvel ; that the system of permitting conscripts to serve one year only, instead of two, provided a certain stage of knowledge has been reached, has greatly quickened the intellectual life of the country, and hence the marvellous results achieved. It is true that this system is a great factor in the industrial development of the conntry,inasmuch as it has produced an educated proletariat. And coupled with this is the fact that the highest educa- tional standard can be attained by any child with- out its costing the parents a single penny. But the keen competition for work among those thus endowed who have not been fortunate enough to make special inventions, has had the result of reducing their wages to almost the same level as those of unskilled work- men, and has thus made it possible to employ highly trained men in industries which otherwise could never afford the services of men educated as at Oxford and Cambridge. But this only gees to show that, at present at all events, the gain is rather a one-sided one. Others, again, tell us that it is the nationalisation of railways, canals, post offices and telegraphs, etc., and the existence of Free Trade within this mighty Empire of sixty million inhabitants, that works this wonder. It is true that the marvellously cheap parcels post, which allows an eleven-pound parcel to be sent fifty miles for 3d. and throughout Germany, Austria, and Hungary for 6d., has resulted in building up a great "direct from producer to con- sumer" food and clothing trade, thus eliminating the middle man. And it is true that the profits derived from these State monopolies go in reduction of taxi- tion. But these splendid institutions have been in existence for some forty years past, and taken alone can hardly, therefore, account for the great increase in prosperity of late years and for the enormous rise in wages during the last decade. Mr.llorsfall, again, will tell us that the wonder is due to the Bnrgermeister, State-paid and appointed for life, with his building plan of the town worked out by his staff years before building actually commences. Bat even the most gifted ruler of even such a little republic as a German town represents, could not do much without money. Whence, then, do the German towns derive the revenue to meet —not only without crippling trade, industry, and enterprise, but on the contrary appar- ently stimulating them —the great cost of the splendid public improvements and public services with which they abound?

"frhe fact is that since the German Government set nine years ago the example of levying a tax of 6 per cent. on the capital value of the laud (in the newly- acquired colony of Kiautchon) ene town after another in Germany itself has adopted the same enlightened system of raising revenue ; and so fruitful a source of revenue has land values proved, the towns, in spite of ever increasing municipal expenditure, I have been able to remit all other direct taxation. The rate of the land values tax is in most cases very small —from 0.2 per cent. to 0.5 per cent. of the selling value of the land. But the tax is levied on all land, whether put to tax or not; and since upwards of 300 towns—amongst them nearly all the large towns, including Berlin, Breslau, Cologne, Frankftirt-on- Maine, Dusseldorf, Hanover, Magdeburg, Charlotten- burg; Essen, Stettin, Konigsberg,Duisberg, Dortmund: Halle, Altona, Kiel, Elberfeld, Danzig, Bremen, Aix- la-Chapelle, Posen, Kassel, etc.—have adopted this system of taxation, the result has been to discourage the holding up of land and to open up to both labour and capital farther avenues of employment. As a matter of fact, hardly any unemployed are to be found at present in Germany ; and a scarcity of labour has set in that seriously troubles many an employer. In addition to the tax on the selling value of the land many German towns have also introduced an "unearned increment tax" similar to the additional) tax of 33 per cent, to be paid when the land changes hands, imposed by the Government in Kiautchou. Hamburg, in fact, is the only one of the bigger towns in Germany that has not yet introduced the unearned increment tax." Recently the Hamburg American Line had to pay £BO,OOO for a site needed for the extension of their offices. The same site changed hands some years ago for £35,000; BO that if Hamburg had had in operation an" unearned incre- ment tax " of 20 per cent, the tax would have yielded on this one item a revenue of £9,000. In order to arrive at the true value of the lani for the ordinary land value tax in ,Kiantchou, the Government requires each owner to give his own valuation every three years, undervaluation being very effectively checked by empowering the Govern- ment to take the land over at the owner's valution if too low. The " unearned "—or, rather, the com- munity-earned-r-increment tax in the German towns is progressive, though it seldom exceeds 25 per cent. of the increased value. But it operates as a great check on land monopoly, and, pending the growth of the land value tax into the Single Tax, it is undonbtedly a sound method of intercepting fir public purposes a part of the huge emoluments derived from land value;. In the German colonies the principle that the land belongs to the nation is still more widely recognised; and of late a law has been passed requir- ing the landlords along the line of route of a railway not only to give the land for the permanent way and the stations free of charge, but also to contribute to the cost of construction of the railway and the stations. If we ask why it is that the teachings of Henry George have first come to fruition, so far as Europe is concerned, in a country with a different language from that in which "Progress and Poverty" was written, we find that the reasons are manifold. In the first place the far superior education of the mass of the people allows of economic issues being much more readily grasped. Then there is the fact that everyone reads his daily paper (the Post Office distributes daily papers everywhere at a cost of I sd. per quarter) ; and, last but by no means least, is the fact that no less than 1,500 towns aril villages in Germany still own, and have owned right down from the middle ages, so much common land that, their inhabitants pay neither rates nor taxes. Five hundred of these townships and villages derive so great a rental from their lands that they able in addition to pay every citizen on New Year's Day a I bonus of from £5 to £2O, as his share of the surplus revenue. Further, the fact that crime is considerably less in those counties which possess the greatest percentage of common land, and that wages also are considerably higher than in those districts possessing no common land, makes it very manifest how much of wisdom lies in the ancient law of our Teutonic ancestors that the land belongs to the people and can never by any moral right become private property. Hohenzollern possesses the greatest extent o

common land, then Hessen and Hanover; Branden- berg, Silesia, Preussen, and Posen the least. For every 100,000 adults in 1901 there were punished for theft in— Hohenzollern 80 Silesia 283 Hessen 152 East Preussen 307 Hanover 168 West Preussen 331 Brandenburg 228 Posen 367. (without Berlin) While wages rule as follows : Hohenzollern 2s. 2d. Silesia is. Hessen 2s. Old. East Preussen is. sd. Hanover is. lid. WestPreussen is. 7d.

Alongside the movement for the taxation of land values there has grown up in Germany since 1900 the more or less frequent custom of letting town lands on " hereditary lease" for seventy years, at the expia- tion of which the whole of the property including improvements reverts to the township, which as a rule pays the out-going tenant two-fifths of the value of the improvements. This system has sprung up as a result of false reports as to the benefits alleged to follow from the leasehold system in London, the Ilgreatest city in the world. But it will necessarily lead to the same jerry-building and scamped work as is so common in this country. Though it must be remembered that in England it is the landlord, and not the community, to whom the prop3rty reverts on the expiration of a lease, and that the landlord pays not a single penny for the improvements that fall into his bands.

G. BERNARD SIIAN\,. The Science of the production and distribution of wealth is political economy. Socialism appeals to that science, and, turning on individualism its own guns, routs it in incurable disaster. * * SIDNEY WEBB: The steady increase of the Government regulation of private enterprise, the growth of municipal administration, and the rapid shifting of the burden of taxation directly to rent and interest, mark in treble lines the Statesman's abandonment of the old Individualism, and our irresistible glide with collec- tivist Socialism.

WILLIAM CLARKE, M.A.

The answer of Socialism to the Capitalist is that society can do without him, just as society now does without the slave-owner or feudal lord, bath of whom were formerly regarded as necessary to the well-being anrA even the very existence of society. In organizing its own business for itself, society can employ, at whatever rate of remuneration may be needed to call forth their powers, those capitalists who are skilled organizers and administrators. But those who are mere dividend-receivers will no longer be permitted to levy a contribution on labour, but must earn their living by useful industry as other and better pople have to do.

SYDNEY OLIVIER.

The apology for individualist appropriation is exploded by the logic of the facts of communist,pro- duction : no man can pretend to claim the fruits of his own labour; for his whole ability and opportunity for working are plainly a vast inheritance and contribution of which he is but a transient and accidental beneficiary and steward ; and his power of turning them to his own account depends entirely upon the desires and needs of other people for his services.

Although, therefore, the German Boden-reformers can hardly be called Single Taxers, it is to their credit that they have initiated and pushed forward with no small measure of success part of the great funda- mental reform advocated by the Prophet of Sans Francisco; and, by affording a practical object lesson of the great and far-reaching effects of even a small tar on land values in promoting the general progress and prosperity of a country, they have earned the gratitude, not only of their Fatherland, but of the whole civilised world, whioh is groan- ing and staggering under the huge deadweight of the old Roman law of private property in land. They have, inieed, given a great incentive to other countries to follow the same line of reform; and it may well be that these other countri 35, having fewer landlords to overcome, or enjoying other mire favour- able conditions, may overtake Germany on the pathway of reform, and that their example may in turn serve to quicken tl4e progress of the very country which first in Europe put into practical effect the gospel according to lEfenly George, and serve also to show how to remedy the few mistakes by reason of over-zeal in starting.

R. OCKEL.

NO TESTS!

TRIUNING COLLEGES FREED FROM SECT RESTRICTIONS.

Emancipated Teachers.

True to the promise he gave in the House o Commons recently, Mr. McKenna, President of the Board of Education, has in his new regulations for the training of teachers embodied rules having for their object the freeing of the training colleges from denominational tests. The new regulations provide that in and after 1903 admission to all training colleges and hostels shall be on the condition that , in no circumstances may the application of a candidate be rejected on the ground of religious faith or by reason of his refusal to undertake to attend or abstain from attending any place of religious worship,or any religious observance, i or instruction in religious subjects n the college or elsewhere.

- It is further provided that after the Ist of August, 190 7, no institution not already recognised as a train- ing college will be so recognised unless it complies with certain conditions as to freedom from denomina- tional restrictions or requirements, and that after the same date no institution not already recognised as a hostel will be so recognisel unless it both complies with these conditions and is also connected with a training college which complies with them. The conditions provide that the instrument, of whatever nature,under which the college or hostel is governesl (a) must not requr,re any members of the teaching staff to belong or not to belong to, any particular denomination ; . _

(b) must not require a mijority of the governing body (whether in virtue of their lenure of any other office or otherwise) to belong or not to belong to any particular religious denomination; and (c) must not provide for the appointment of a majority of the Governing Body by any pers-m or persons who, or by any body the mjaority of whom are required (whether in virtue of their tenure of any other office or otherwise) to belong or not to belong to any particular religious denomination.

===== 0002601_19070713_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
RELIGIOUS INSTRUCTION

In colleges or hostels recognised for the first time after Ist August, 1907, fl) catechism or formulary distinctive of any particular religious denomination may be taught, except in cases where the parent or guardian of any student requests the Governors in writing to provide for the student religious instruc- tion in the doctrines, catechism, or formularies dis- tinctive of any particular denomination. In such cases the Governors may, if they think fit, and if the instrument under which the training college or hostel is governed requires or 'does not prohibit the giving of such instruction in the college or hostel, comply with such request and provide such instruction accordingly out of funds other than grants made by the Board of Education or by any local authority. In order to enforce the Conscience Clause effectively examination of candidates by the college authorities ' as a condition of admission is to be prohibited. Hence- forth all students entering training colleges must give an undertaking to serve in an approved school for seven years in the case of men and five years in the case of women. Non-compliance with this under- taking will involve a refund to the Board of Educa- tion of sums proportional to the number of obligatory yelrs not served.

VICAR KILLED IN A HAYFIELD.

- ------ ------ --- -- 1 The Rev. W. Kewley, a few months ago appointed to the living of Millom, was killed in a hayfield on Wednesday evening. Men were loading hay from a field near the vicarage, when one of the carts com- menced to travel backwarls down a slope. The rev. gentlemen ran to stop the vehicle, but it knocked him down, one of the wheels passing over his chest.

GRAHAM WALLAS.

Socialism offers a new and nobler life, when full sympathy with those about them, springing from full knowledge of their condition, shall be a source of happiness, and not, as now, of constant sorrow—when it shall no longer seem either folly or hypocrisy for a a man to work openly for his highest ideal.

ANNIE BESANT If there is one vice more certain than another to be unpopular in a Socialist community, it is laziness.

HUBERT BLIND. Enquiry proves that Socialism is founded upon a triple rock, historical, ethical, and economic. It gives, to those who make it, a great hope—a hope which, once it finds entrance into the heart of man, stays to soften life and sweeten death. By the light of the Socialist Ideal he sees the evil—yet sees it pass. Then and now he begins to live the cleaner, braver, holier life of the future; and he marches forward, steeled and stimulated, with resolute step, with stead- fast eye, with equal pulse.

"SOCIAL' REFORM." Sir,—l always read with interest the well-written contributions of " Lazarus " in your "Voice of Labour " column, and his pointed remarks in last week's issue, should claim the serious and sympathetic attention of every intelligent parent, conscientious citizen and patriotic humanitarian. "St. John's Ward," writes " Lazarus," "has a population chiefly composed of labourers and others whose means of livelihood are somewhat precarious —and contains very bad slums, courts alleys and miserable yards -some houses have no back door, and the children have no playground except the school- yard and the streets.' Despite the humanising influence of the compara- tively extensive development of municipal enterprise during the last decade, and the great advance made in that period, both in sanitary science and personal oy giene, this terrible indictment of presant-day social conditions could, alas, be applied with equal truth to the poorer working-class districts in many of our large industrial centres. I am firmly convinced, Sir, that it is utterly impos- sible to appreciably diminish any of the crying social evils from which we, as a community, suffer to-day, until we have obtained a national and practical recog- nition of the following fundamental principles : - I. The c )mpulsory payment of at least a minimum living wage to all adult workers. 2. The granting of Labour's Charter (The Right to Work) to every able-bodied man and woman in the land. _ _ _

The adoption of the first measure would, if strictly enforced, entirely abolish the sweating evil and also materially raise the standard of living to thousands— aye millions—of toilers in the United Kingdom; whilst the passing of the latter reform would simplify the unemployed problem by guaranteeing the public provision of suitable labour for all workless applicants. Why does not the present Liberal Government, with its huge majority and almost unbounded influence, seriously attempt to legislate on these (or similar) lines? What practicable alternative to these suggbs- tions have our local political leaders to offer for the solution of the great poverty problem? Further I unhesitatingly assert that in a civilized country no property owner should be permitted to draw rents from " slums,courts, alleys and houses that have no back dolr." It is the bounden duty of the members of the Warrington Town Council to at once remove these vile dens from our midst, and, on behalf of their fellow-townsmen erect in their stead well- built, healthy dwellings, in every respect worthy of the inhabitants of a great Empire. It is our sacred duty as loyal citizens to strenuously insist that they (OUR representatives) promptly pp-- form these communal services. Will " Lazarus " give us a strong lead ?--Yours etc., DEMOS.

His Honour refused to allow the amendment of the claim.

Mr. W. FL Bellian, motor car and cycle dealer, Warrington, was then called and said that on May Bth, 1906, he was ont driving a motorcar. Mr. Watts objected to witness stating the facts of the accident. All he had to prove was that defendant gave tha order.

Mr. Bellian continuing, said he saw defendant at Walton in an accident. Defendant instructed him to make good any damage that was done to the car and he would pay for it. Mr. Davies and Mr. Smiilham, driver to Mr. Reynolds, were in the car with witness. The repairs were subsequently executed, a new bonnet having to be ordered from Paris. New mudguards had also to be fixed, and it took a man and a boy fifteen hours to straighten up the steering gear and various parts of the' front portion of the car which were slightly put out of truth. The charge for the repairs was £7 10s.,which was only cost price. Witness took the defendant home in the car, and defendant thanked him for his kindness and again stated that whatever the repairs cost,the bill must be sent to him and he would pay. In answer to Mr. Watts, witness admitted receiving a. letter from Mr. Worsley in reply to a reqhest for pay- ment of his account, in which he stated that be was sur- prised at his (Mr. Bellian's) latter as he did not owe him anything, nor had he received the account which was stated to have been rendered. Defentant was dazed after the awident, but it was when he had come round that he gave the order. Witness denied telling defendant's brother that the damage was only about 30s. John Snailham, Risley, motor car driver to Mr. Reynolds' said defendant told Mr. Etonian to make good the damage and he would pay for it. Witness considered the amount charged was reasonable. Rowland \Davies, partner with Mr.Bellian, also stated that he heard defendant give the order. The motor car was at a dead stop when defendant ran into it and the principal damage was done through the defendant fall- ing on to the bonnet. Mr. Watts for the defendant said that if Mr. Worsley said anything about repairing the car, he (Mr. Watts) submitted that he was dazed and did not know what he was saying. Defendant would, however, go into the box and state that the only thing he asked was what damage had been done to the the car. When he received a letter asking him to pay he replied that he did not owe the firm a penny. Rive months afterwards they thought it worth while to put the matter into the hands of Mr. Gittens: who wrote applying .for payment, and defendant replied denying liability. Defendant had never seen Mr. Bellian since the a.ccident,and the only communications he had received were the letters to which he replied as stated. He had never had particulars and had not had a single account .for damage done. It was evident that plaintiffs were in two minds as to whom to sue. What they ought to have done was to bring an action for damages caused through the collision. Defendant had been put to considerable expense for doctor's bill and repairs to his bicycle, and his 'brother bad to repair I his own motor cycle. He (Mr. Watts) submitted th it Plaintiffs themselves were of opinion that it was an accident, and that 05 one was to h,latne, and that they never bad any idea of sending in a bill but for reasons which he could not explain that day. M. Edward Worsley, the defendant, gave evidence and said that he never told Mr. Bellian to put the car in repair, to his kno.eledge. He might possibly while in a dazed condition have told plaintiff that he would pay. He had asked for pirticraars of the claim several times but had not been supplied with them. He never received a detailed account. He was under medical treatment a fortnight. Hugh Worsley, brother of the defendant, said he did not hear his brother tell Mr. Bellian that he would pay for the daniage. He did not, tvevover, hear all the conversation. Defendant was riding an ordinary cycle , and was holding on to witness's shoulder when the accident happened. William Harrison, manaeing clees-.1 to the defendant, sail be had applied to the plaintiff for particulars on two occasions but had not received them. His Honour said three witnesses had sworn to the fact that defendant- and ha would pay for the repairs. Defendant said he did not remember anything about the matter. He (the Judge) believed it was perfectly pmaible that defendant got such a shock that the matter might have passed from his memory. Still it was sworn to by three witnesses, and defendant was not so con- fused that he did not know what. he was doing. He (the Judge) acquitted defendant of all intention of misleading ,or deceiving the Court. The matter evidently had passed from his mind. Mr. Watts very properly complained that particulars had not been furnished but _details of claim could always be had on application to the Court. The fact that the account was for £7 108. was known to the defendant for some I time. He was satisfied that the £7 103. did not contain any profit whatever, but was simply the cost of the repairs, and he gave judgment for the plaintiffs for that amount with costs.

Local Journalists at Llandudno. . The quarterly meeting of the Liverpool District of the Institute of Journalists was held on SAturday at the Llandudno Town Hall, through the courtesy of the Mayor. Mr. A. H Ward-Jones, V.P., chairman of the district, presided, and representatives were present from Liverpool, North Wales, Warrington, Wrexham, and other sub-districts. Business of special interest to journalists was dealt with, including a scheme which has been drafted with a view to making the institute more effective in promoting the ,practical welfare of the members of the profession. In appeal was made for increased aid on behalf of the Orphan Fund, which is supporting several children in the district. Messrs. J. H. Quail (Liverpool), 11. Isher- woed (Warrington\ J. W. Jones (Rhyl), and J. H. Fitzpatrick (Wrexham), were appointed delegates to the annual conference, which will be held at Scar- 'borough in September.

Theft of Slates. At the Daresbury Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, William Jackson was charged with having on the 19th May, 1906, stolen 84 slates and a piece of timber, of the value of 43., the property of Frederick Wm. Wright, builder, St. Mary's-street, Latchford. The slates and timber, it Is.a; stated, were left in, a field near the house, and they were subsequently missed. Information was given .to the police, inquiries were made, and the slates and timber were found in Mr. Elijah Connington's yard, the articles having been sold to him by the prisoner. The prisoner was formerly in Mr. Wright's employ: Prisoner said a man natnefl Jones asked him to buy the slates, and he went to see Mr. Cunnington as a probable purchaser. Ile believed the slates belonged to Jones' and not to Mr. Wright. Tbe Bench did not accept the explana- tion, and committed prisoner to goal for a month with hard labour.

The amateur athletic sports - the tenth of its kind —in aid of the Wallasey medical charities took place on Monday evening at the Stanley Athletic Grounds, in the presence of a fair assemblage of spectators. - The principal attraction was the five miles scratch bicycle race for the Wallasey Licensed Victuallers' Challenge Cup (value 25 guineas). The holder was C. Berry (Salford Harriers), of Warrington, tvllo again competed. The entries were more numerous than was expected, and with the view of rendering the final effort more interesting it was decided to run two preliminary heats of t.tvo miles each, the first, second, and third winners iv each heat to engage in the five mile contest. Owing to the heavy rain the track was by no means in the best of condition, but, despite this circumstance, the six riders who took part in the final were responsible for an,interesting race, which proved highly exciting towards the close, Ben Jones, of Wigan, securing a brilliant victory. Berry was second, and T. M. Dobson of Preston third. Time 15mins. 19 2-ssecs. P.S.A. Meetings.

' There was a fiiir attendance at the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday. Mr. G. Smiley was the chairman and he took for his subject" Back- sliding." He showed how departing from qunday Schools and churchwork meant unhappiness to the backslider, and strongly appealed to him to return to his Heavenly Father's fold. Their Father had enough and to spare. Service for the Master was the surest way of keeping then from backsliding. Mr. W. Light was the speaker. His subject was: "The City four square, the Kingdo n of Heave..." He appealed to men not to be narrow-minded in their beliefs. The different sects of religion were all striving for eternity with the Father, but they preferred different ways of going to the end. "The City four square " had its doors open to all comers. He could see in imagina- tion, said the speaker, the great intellectual giants of the age passing through the city, also through the gate of love, Florence Nightingale and Grace Darling, who did so much for humanity. The city was heaven for all, not merely for a, few narrow-minded people. Let them see to it that in the last day they might all I be feund entering in at the gates of the 'City four I square." Messrs. R. Wood and Noden rendered two duets, "Ora pro nobis" and "Beyond," in a very effective manner. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. LA DIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Adtv. 1 The Manchester and Liverpool D:strict Banking Co., Ltd. In their report to be presented at the meeting of shareholders to be held at the head office, Spring 1 Gardens, Manchester, on Wednesday next, the directors state that the gross profit for the half-year ending; 29th June last, after providing for all bad and doubi- ful debts, is £235,823 12s. 7d., to which has to be added the amount brought forward from 31st December last, viz., £41,110 7s. id., making a total of £276,933 19s. Bd. From this sum the following deductions have to be made: Total expenses at the head office, London Office, and Branches, including salaries, stationery, taxes, &c., £83,909 ss. 9d.; half-year's provision for income tax, £5,000; leaving a net profit of £188,024 13s. 11d. The directors recommend the declaration of a dividend of 21s. per share, which will take £131,250, leaving a balance of £56,774 13s. 11d. Of this it is proposed to carry to the Reserve Fudd £lO,OOO, to Pension Fund £5,000, and to next account £41,774 13s. 11d. The Directors regret the continued decline in the price of Consols and of other gilt-edged securities. They adhere to the policy, always hitherto followed by the Bank, of valuing the Bank's securities in the Balance Sheet at or below the matket prices. For this purpose they have now taken £185,000 :from the Reserve Fund, which, with the addition of •110,000 ; now made, will still stand at the ample figure of i £1,670,000. They have valued the Bank's • large I holding of Consols at .£B2, and they would point out that the earning power of the investments is in no way diminished by the decline in their market prices. The property of the Bank is being constantly improved and added to, but still stands in the Balance sheet at £300,000, which is very mulch below its real value. The Directors regret to intitnate that Mr:George Gorden ! Warren, a member of the *Board since 1873, died on 21st March last, and that Mr. George Lewis ceases to.: be a Director, through unavoidable absence, conse- quent on continued ill-health. Diu ing the past half ' year offices have been opened at Whitchurch, Prest- wich, Upholland, Goostrey, Crewe Cattle Market, ! Mottram-in-Longdendale, Broadbottom, and Calcheth, 1 near Warrington. I

PENKETH.

GARDEN PARTY.—Saturday last was a red-letter day in the calendar of the members of the Penketh and Sankey Weil Union. The President, Mr. J. B. Aitken, gave the members a cordial invitation to a garden party at his residence, and the members to the number of 30 availed themselves of the kind invitation. The party assembled at 3 p.m. and were warmly welcomed by Mr. and Mrs. Aitken and invited to amuse themselves with croquet, ieic., until tea time. Tea was a popular item on the programme, and was presided over by tbr hopt and hostess, and the Vicar, the Rev. J. R. Jones, M.A. Prior to sitting down to tea, the ;Vicar arranged the , party on the lawn and took a photograph. At the con- clusion of the repast, the Vicar moved a vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Aitken for their hospitality and spoke in high terms of Mr. Aiken's support of such institu- tions as would tend to uplift the menknd women of the village. He hpped the members would keep together end make the cht,ir one to be proud of.- Mr. D. Dunn, the conductor of the choir, seconded the motion, and hoped the choir would grow in numbers, so that they could attempt more ambitious works than they were at present doingl'he party then adjourned to the lawn,and guider the conductorship of Mr. Dunn sang several male voice songs which were creditably rendered and much enjoyed by other guest.. Bowls and other games then occupied the time until darkness put an end to a very pleasant day. As the choir were leaving,thg paused at the hall door and sang one or two short pieces, which in the stillness of the evening had a very charming effect, FRIENDLY SOCIETIES' SPORTS.—On Thursday evening the Yriendly Societies held their sports on the Recrea- tier/Ground. These sports should have taken place on .Tune 15th, the day the Friendly Societies held their ,rlemonstration, but owing to the wet weather they had to be postponed. The members of the different societies formed a procession facing the Post Office at 7 p.m., headed by the Penketh Tannery Prize Band,and marched to the ground. The sports commenced at 7.30 p.m.,each event being well contested. The first race was 100 yards for ages from 16 to 30. and was won by-I,E.Darlington, 2, J. Bowyer • 3, Fl. MolyneauK. One hundred yards, ag,es 30 upwards (two prizes) : 1, F.Dale; 2, J.Pearson. Hundred yards race for the -hand :-1, W. Ray ; 2, J. Bowyer; 3, S. Tunstall. The evening was brought to a close with holding a veteran's race which was won by the village post-master, IVlr.D.Houghtori. Thanks are due to the weather clerk for allowing a fine evening, which brought a great number of spectators to watch the events.

UNEXPECTED CALLS

summer brings many unexpected demands upon the cook. Extra puddings and cakes have frequently to be made at short notice,and the thrifty housewife looks ahead, and lays in a reserve stock of necessaries. Among the most essential of these are currants. No practical cook needs to be,reminded that the morning's baking Is sadly hampered if the currant jar is found to be empty.

HOLIDAY COMPETITION ESSAYS

Dear Uncle Ned,—l would rather stay at a farmhouse in the ,sweet country than at the seaside. It is lovely when one wakes up in the morning to see the pretty-- roses or honey- suckle peeping in through the window. Then there .are the long walks through the shady lanes to be enjoyed, and the romping in the hay. It is rare fun, too, to ,set off, armed with crooks, to gather nuts or blackberries. It is lovely on a hot, sultry day to sit under the trees and enjoy Nature's wonderful beauty. I prefer the glorious country raiher than •the noisy seaside. ELSIE LEWIS.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l think I would like to spend my holidays in the country the best. Of course, it is very pleasant by the seaside. but better in the country. It. is • nice to see the women milk the cows and feed the pigs. fowls, and geese. Flowers decorate the fields and hedges, and everything seems pleasant. You can chase the geese until you get -very thirsty, and when you are thirsty you can soon get a glass of nice milk. You look healthy, and get well sunburnt, after a pleasant time spent in the country. FLORENCE DARK.

I like spending my holidays at the seaside best. Early in the morning you can go down by the seaside with your fpades and pails and make sand castles and paddle about, and have, oh! such games and fun. My schoolmaster took us once to Yarmouth, and we did lave some fun. We had dinner and tea there. We ho.lie at one o'clock next morning. It is itiCO to see the pleasure boats and the people c:,,tching slahnps and other fishes.

WIN.NIE ELLIOTT,

Conscience does make cowards of some men, when tempted to do what they know to be wrong. But it also makes others true to their ideas of right and wrong, even when pain or death may be the price of obedience. a The knight of Elizabeth's Court who was without fear or reproach was Sir Philip Sydney. All know the story of his heroic self-denial, as he lay bleeding on the field of Zutphen. A ser- vant had brought him a small flask of wine, which Sydney was about drinking just as a wounded soldier was being carried by. Seeing the longing look which the man cast on the liquor, Sydney ordered it to be given him, say- ing, "Poor fellow! your need is greater than :uine." No one can read this anecdote—it can- not be too often repeated to youth—without ad- miring Sydney's adherence to the chivalrous principles in which he had been trained. Be

was a Christian and an English gentlematl, and must of necessity be true to hi knightly cow science. Turn from that piciare to this: A high-caste Sepoy, one of those natbe soldiers whom England trains to fight her battles in India, lay dying on the battle-field of thirst and loss" of blood. An English officer, going alone over the field, found him far apart from his comrades and offered him a drink of water. " My caste, sirl my •caste!" said the Sepoy, faintly. "We are alone; no one will see you," answered the officer, pressing the water on him. The Sepoy shook his head, and said, "God sees me 1" The man was a heathen, and had been reared in a system which taught him that his caste must be religiously kept from taint. To accept water from European hands would stain his caste and be a sin. Intense as was his thirst, weak as was his 4 body through loss of blood, he chose to endure rather than prove false to his convictions. A conscience held by religious conviction and love of God will hold a man under temptation when nothing else will.

MAKING TEA.

When tea was first brought into England a !)ound was given to a poor woman. Not know- ing what to do with it, she put the tea leaves in a saucepan and put hot water over them. cv'hen it had boiled, she threw the liquid away. She then thought that she had tasted many ;.)etter things than tea. _

QUESTION AND ANSWER. " Hello ! Pat, I hear your dog, is dead." " It is." " Was it a lapdog?" " Yes, it would lap anything." -" What did it die of ?" , "It died of a Tuesday." •' I mean, how did it die?" " it died on its back." "I mean. how did the dog meet its death?" " It didn't meet its death; Its death overtook

•' I want to know what was the complaint ?" "No complaint; every one for miles round .tenled to be satisfied." "I wish to know how did it occur?" "The dog was no cur; he was a thorough- )rc:l animal."

"Tell me, what disease did the dog die of?" "He went to fight a circular saw." " What was the result ?" "The dog only lasted one round." HARRY Copp.

Barber: "This is a bad shilling, sir." Customer: "That's all right. I had a bad have."

Papa: "Can't you remember where I left my eye-glasses this morning?" Dora: "No, I can't." Papa: "It is really marvellous the forgetful_ ness of you girls."

PROUD KATE. • Her frock is gay, her gloves are new, She holds a dainty flower; Poor ragged Ben has swept away The mud left by a shower; No thanks has he from haughty Kate, He thinks—but not aloud— "You're pretty, miss, but spoil it all, Because you look so proud."

CYCLISTS AT BEESTON. ,

Warrington Club's President's Day.

1 The members of the Warrington Cycling Club on Saturday paid their annual visit to Beeston Towers, the residence of their president, Mr. John Naylor. For years past the cyclists have been favoured with i charming weather for this, the most popular fixture of I the year, but onSaturday they were not so fortunate. ! It has bee ome a point of honour that for "President's Day" as it is known, there shall be a good muster and this year there was no decrease in numbers, , though probably more than usual sought the comfort i of the railway in preference to making the journey awheel. Those who proceeded by road had not the most enjoyable of rides, several heavy showers causing them to seek shelter on the way. At Beesion Towers i the time was pleasantly spent n the gardens and on the croquet and tennis lawns and • the bowling green. Mr. Naylor's hospitality has become pro- verbial and on Saturday he added to his reputation as a host. Owing to the unsettled weather tea was served in the coach house instead of on the lawn and full justice was done to an excellent repast. The annual concert followed in the handsome billiard room Sergeant Major Delmage presiding in the absence Of the Captain, Mr. F. Fieldhoase. The club numbers in its ranks gentlemen of no mean ability as entertainers and on this occasion all acquitted themselves well. A glee party, consisting of Messrs. Hassan, Corrin, Clay, and Gough, sang very well, their voices blending splendidly in "Soldier's fare- well," "In absence," and " Comrades in arms." Messrs. B. J. Poole, Hassall, Clay, and Gough delighted all with the manner in which they rendered their respective songs, and the duets by Mr. Hassall and Mr. Gough and by Mr. Clay and Mr. Corrin were warmly applauded. Mr. Gorman contributed humorous songs with effect. Mr. Anderson acted as accompanist. Before the party dispersed advantage was taken of an opportunity to thank Mr. and Mrs. Naylor for their hospitality. Sergeant-Major Delmage said that in the absence of their popular and worthy Captain (Mr. F. Fieldhouse). it was his pleasing duty to thank Mr. and Mrs. Naylor, on behalf of the members of the Cycling Club, and those ladies who, through their kindness, had been enabled to attend that pleasant function. They hoped Mr. and Mrs. Naylor would realise how thoroughly they appreciated their great kindness in inviting them annually to meet them and their family at what had been so aptly described by their worthy secretary, Mr.l G. H. Smith, as" your beautiful home, Beeston Tower." (Applause). To Mrs. Naylor in particular their 'thanks were due for the sumptuous manner in which they had all been enabled to regale their inner man during their visit. They had intended at a later date during the I present year to celebrate the coming-of-age of the club and hoped for the pleasure of their company, and also many of their friends and trusted that on the occasion ithey might to some extent be able to afford Mr. ,and Mrs. Naylor as pleasurable a time as they had all had that day and lge further trusted that their little musical programme had met with appreciation. The members of the club were very grateful to those artistes who not only on that special occasion, but also on so many other occasions. afforded them so much pleasure by their vocal and instrumental performances, and he now on behalf of the club wished Mr. and Mrs. Naylor and family "long life, happiness, and the best of luck," and trusted they might all have the pleasure of meeting at very many more such pleasant functions, as the present had undoubtedly been, during the many years which he sincerely hoped were before them all. He had received the following telegram from the captain of the club : —:" Delmage, c/o Naylor, Beeston Towers, Cheshire. Just arrived Stroniness. Stormy passage. Hope Naylor's weather with Iron. Fieldhouse, Garnett and Valentine." He had much pleasure in asking their worthy Secretary (Mr. George H. Smith) to support his remarks. (Applause.) Mr. Smith said he did so with much • pleasure.- It was hardly necessary for him to tell Mr. and Mrs. Naylor how much the members of the club enjoyed these visits. The fact of the large attendance showed that they were appreciated very highly. He believed he was right in saying that their first visit to Beeston Towers was in the year 1896. There had been numerous changes since that time, but there were two things which had not changed. The first was the warmth of the rectption by Mr. and Mrs. Naylor, and second, the heartiness with which the members of the club accepted the kind invitation extended to them from year to year. They had now nearly completed 21 years of their exist- ence as a club, and they had the satisfaction of knowing that after• all that time they were numerically and socially stronger than ever. (Applause). They ' had a membership of 132, and their numbers were still increasing, showing that as a body they had plenty of life and energy still left in tbem—(applause)--although some cyclists ..said that club life so far RS cycling was concerned was dead. (Laughter.) On the contrary they might claim without egotism that they were one of the strongest clubs in the North of England. At the recent War- rington Infirmary bazaar and fête, held at Hill Cliffe, they were responsible for-the care of the out-door amusements, and he mentioned that fact to show that when a responsibility rested upon the members it was conscientiously discharged. and such a handsome sup- port was given that the arrangements were brought to a successful issue, to the credit of the club and all ccncerned (Applause.) The motion was carried with much heartines. Mr. Naylor, in reply, said he had gone through the or deal of replying to a similar resolution many timer. Mrs. Naylor, who was a better speaker than he was, at any rate privately—(laughter—should have answered for them both. Ji king apart, h?, was much obliged for the kind vote of thanks, and he could assure them that it was always a pleasure to him to entertain the mem- berc of the club at Beeston Towers. He trusted that all the ladies and gentlemen hs.d thorcughly enjoyed themselves. Air. Na3lor then commented upon the use of the roads by motorists and cycl'sts, with special reference to the question of the dust pre blem. The fault, in his opinion, rested with the mitnner in which the roads were constructed, but he hor ed the County Council would not rest until they bad discovered some method of effectually disposing of the question. (Hear, hestr),

" Por he's a jolly good fellow" was heartily sung and the visitors set out upon the return journey, Warrington being again reached about ten o'clock.

CRUSHED UNDER SEVERAL TONS.

A terrible accident occurred on Tuesday afternoon the premises of the Yorkshire Engine Company, at, Wincobank, Sheffield. Whilst some men were erecting an electric overhead travelling crane one of them a wheelwright, named Thomas Clarke, fell from a girder forty feet from the ground. No sooner bad the unfor- tunate man slipped than the 4hole mass of mechanism, weighing several tons, toppled over and completely crushed Clarke, who died shortly after being extricated from the debris. Clatke was a married man with a family, and was about 50 years of age.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR,

The Art Master. Sir,—l was very pleased to find in your last issue that attention had at last been called to the unfortunate mistake made by the Education Stzb-committee,relating to the appointment of headmaster at the Warrington School of Art. I have watched with pleasure the exceedingly good work done by Mr. Pendlebury since his appointment as Assistant Master, and excellent reports have reached me from students under his care, many of the latter to whom I have spoken, being very much annoyed at the , line of action taken by the sub-committee. It appears to me that Mr. Pendlebury's qualifications and testimonials could hardly have been read (or such a mistake could not have been made) oc his past services to the school considered. It is not often that a Committee has a chance of securing a master with such wideexperience in teach- ing as Mr. Pendlebury, to say nothing of his having been an Official Examiner in Art to the Board of Educa- tion itself for about 7 years ! I know of one " selected " cauEldate who said he would refuse to accept the post if appointed, when he heard that Mr. Pendkbrr ir was already in the school, and adding that it would jbe very difficult to find a better man. Hoping that when the matter comes before the full Education Committee they will see their way to reconsider the appointment, anti thus do justice to one who has for many years brought honour and credit to his native town.-1 remain, yours faithfully, JAMES BENNETT. Lynton Grange, Padgate, \Carrington, July 10th, 1907.

Socialism and the Teachirgs of Christ. Reply to ohn Workman. Sir,--It is often said you can prove anything from the Bible. But this is only when a faulty method of , interpretation is used, and especially when men go to the Scriptures not for guidance' in the search of truth, but to support some belief they have already adopted. I leave your readers to judge whether or no " John Workman's" interpretation of the parable of the labourers is not a case in point. He says :—" If you remember the parable of the labourers in the vineyard, Jesus Christ there taught alosson which I think showed on what system work is to be paid for. Each man received according to his coeds." Each man did not so receive ; the need of each man differs according to the capacity of his stomach, his brain,' the number of his family, &c. In this case each man received an equal amount, viz. one penny. There is nothing about needs in the parable. What justification did the householder give for his unusual conduct when the labourers came murmuring? He said, "'Friend I do thee no wrong, didst thou not agree with me for a penny. Take that thine is and go thy way, I will give unto the last even as unto thee ?" In theselast words we have the claim clearly laid down of individual possession of capital and the right to its free use. This is the right that the Socialists question, claiming the right of State appro- priation, without compensation, the disinheriting of the individual both of his property and his right of administration. It wonld he in accord with the teach- ings of the parable for me to say that Jesus Christ emphasized by this parable the right of the individual to use his capital as he thought •well, "to do what I will with mine own." But I have no wish to press this point,the whole con- text removes the parable from any possible application to our industrial system. Christ is here illustrating a deep spiritual truth telling us what "the kingdom of heaven is like unto:" "John Workman" commends me to a lady's quota- tion from Kipling's "L' Envoi"in which his tired painter looks forward to the next life. " When no one shall work for money, and no one-shall work for fame, but each for the joy of working, &e." Whatever be the conditions in the next life there is no present prospect of work in this life being unnecessary to enable us to earn our daily bread, and this will ever have to be done by the sweat of brow or brain. To expect any other condition is as futile as to hope for the realiza- tion of another of ICipling's • fancies contained in the same poem where he tells us that as painters" We shall splash on a ten leagued canvas, with brushes of comet's hair." As long as toil isloborious, and involves con- tinned sacrifice of ease and comfort, surely we are justi- fied in protesting against any system, however alluring it may appear from some points of view, which denies to the individual the legitimate and free use of the fruits of his labour. "John Workman" tells us that the nationalization of the means of production is a natural and logieal sequence to the nationalizing of natural elements. Here we come to the vital point of difference between myself and the Socialists,and on which I would join issue. I claim that a man shonld have the fruit of his labour, the whole fruit, and nothing but the fruit. If an individual appropriates national elements such as land, and thereby is in a position to secure value which he has done nothing to create, this value being the result of the labour and association of others. then he wrongs his fellows. If on the other hand the State by appropriation,without compensation, takes of the fruit of the toil of the individual then a wrong is done to the individual, and the deei is no 'less immoral because it is done under the authority of the State, and not by an individual. Can" John Workman " not see that the two things are fundamentally different, one being the gift • of the Creator, not to the few, but to all (unless we accuse the Almighty of favouritism). The other is the result of individual effort, which if appropriated will deaden the springs from which this effort takes its rise. I am well aware that private ownership of such things as land has been recognised by 'the State, that the savings perhaps of a lifetime have been invested in land. Those facts make the problem of the State resuming the ownership (which it ought never to have parted with) immensely difficult and well-nigh impracticable. It will however have to be done,probahly by gradual steps. What is wanted is a clear recognition of what constitutes individual rights and possession, and what are the rights and possessions which the State ought to hold in trust for the benefit of all. The legislation for years past in Ireland and the Land Billsproposed for both England and Scotland seem to recognise the wrongs arising from unrestricted individual ownership in land, and various limitations Of owner's rights are proposed. Probably if all our Senators appreciated the essential difference between the title individuals have to what they have done nothing to make, and the titles others have to what they have themselves made and saved, they wotild proceed by bolder and surer steps towards the acquisition of those things for which the State ought to act as trustee. There are several points in "John Workman's " letter in which the meaning seems to me very obscure, and in , conclusion especially the part dealing with Churches, Christianity, and Socialism. If I gather the drift of his reasoning aright it is something like this, if we, as Christians, lived up to the faith we professed we should become Socialists or Fabians, indeed that Socialism is • Christianity embodied in practice. I have no wish to put a meaning to "John Workman's " words which he would not wish to convey, and fie can let us know if I have misunderstood But on the other hand if broadly I have inter- - preted his faith I should be glad if he would tell us how Socialism, not as an vague ideal merely, but as a -definite constructive industrial policy, harmonises with ~tae teachings of Christ.

CHARLES E. PARKER.

-3t. Andrews, July 10th, 1907.
