RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present Mr. T. Baxter (presiding). Admiral Jones Parry, Messrs. J. Spruce, W. M. Morris, P. Booth, P. Collins, T. Wright, J. Mynekyme, J. Hallows, T. Guest, G. Pilling, J. Douglas, A. R. Norman. A. Haining, J. L. Jones, J. H. Davies. A. H. Talbot, R. Nixon G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk),T. Cook, W. R. De Prez and J. Pearson (relieving officers) and J. Cunnah (master). Admiral Jones Parry moved " That with a view of encouraging boys and girls in the Workhouse to remain in the country, a small dairy be established for, say six cows as soon as a field or fields can be secured." They were often asked why the boys from the Workhouse would not settle in the country and he believed that the only way to get them to do so would be to teach them farming and interest them in the work. The same thing applied to the girls. The proposal would not cost the Board much and would be a step in the righ direction.

_ Mr. J. L. Jones seconded. Mr. Spruce supported. He thought a dairy could be established with great advantage all round. Mr. Morris moved that the question be adjourned as there was only a very small attendance at the meeting. Mr. Douglas seconded the amendment. Mr. Norman said he took it that the resolution meant that a committee would be appointed to enquire into the matter and bring something definite before a future meeting of the Board as to the cost involved, and it would then be for the Board to decide whether to take it up or not. The resolution would not empower the Committee to establish a dairy. He had a great deal of sympathy with the idea of doing all they could for the children, but he was strongly of opinion that before long the State would do something to remove the children from the Workhouse, and in that case he did not think it would be wise to go to any great outlay. If they could got something reasonable it would have his support. Mr. Hallows said he could see no harm in adjourning the matter. Even the formation of a special committee was an important matter. Admiral Parry said there was no harm done by the resolution, but delay was fatal, and nothing could be gained by an adjournment. They were not at that meet- hag being asked to spend one penny. 1 Mr. Talbot said that personally he was not in favour of the scheme. He thought it was going to cost the Board a great deal, ,as they would want buildings, machinery, attendants, l&c., and it would be the cost which would block the scheme. It was decided to adjourn the question, the clerk in the meantime to make enquiries from those Boards where dairy's had been established. The Finance Committee imported the payment in out-relief of £6O to Mr. Cook, £3O to Mr.. de Prez, £25 to Mr. Pearson. Mr. Spruce reported that the number of inmates of the house was 141 as compared with 139 twelve months ago. During the fortright 130 vagrants had been relieved, an increase of 64 upon the number during the I corresponding period of last year.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present :—Mr. G. Pilling (presiding), Admiral Jones Parry, Messrs. J. Spruce, P. Booth, T. Wright, J. Mynekyme, J.Hallows,T. Guest, A. R. Norman, A.Hain- ing, J. L. Jones, J. H. Davies, A. H. Talbot, R. Nixon, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors), J. Farrington (inspector), and Dr. Adams (medical officer). The Settle Rural District Council forwarded the following resolution 'That in the opinion of this Council the time has now arrived when mean Toady' should be a national ()harp." They asked the Council to pass a similar resolution and forward it to the Local Government Board. The matter was referred to the Parliamentary Committee. The Clerk intimated that he had received a notice from the Town Clerk of Warrington, intimating the intention of the Corporation to apply for a pro- visional order to supply electricity in the whole of the area of the Runcorn Rural District. Mr. Hallows asked that the matter should be referred to the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee. They protested against the proposal. It was decided to refer the matter to the parishes affected by the order. Dr. Adams presented a report upan the outbreak of diphtheria at Norley. He would, he said, like to remind the Council of the observations made by him in the past on the unsatisfactory condition of the Norley Water Supply, and those remarks might apparently be applicable to the whole of that township. It had always appeared to him that the condition of the water supply might ultimately result in detriment to the health of the inhabitants of the township. Whilst not absolutely condemning the water supply as the source of the outbreak of diphtheria, it was impossible for him to totally dissociate the general prevalence of hard water and of disease in Norley. Since 1905 there had been in Norley 11 cases of scarlet fever and 10 cases of diphtheria. The pecrentage of deaths from diphtheria was terrible, and as six cases out of the ,ten proved fatal. All the cases were offered removal to the Isolation Hospital but only three out of the whole of the patients were removed. It was a matter for very deep regret to him that the people of Norley were hostile to removal of patients, and many, in addition, were equally firm in declining to allow their bedding, clothing, &c., to be disinfected until a death which took place appeared to have an educational value. Mr. Davies said it seemed to him that had water was the cause of the outbreak of diptheria at Norley. The report was approved.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LOCAL SITCCESS.

At the recent examinations held at the Victoria University, Manchester, Mr. A. Gilbert Peake, son of Mr. Arthur Peake, chevaist, of Queen-street, Esrlestown, was successful in passing his first M.B. FLOWER SERVICE AT ST. JOHN'S.

St. John's Sunday Schools held their annual flower services on Sunday. The proceeds are devoted to carrying out the Sunday school work in the parish. The 'Vicar, Rev. T. R. Matthews, at the afternoon service for children, poiated out how flowers helped to brighten the lives of those who were sick and not alp% to help themselves. Each child brought a bouquet of flowers, which were distributed among the sick in the parish and various hospitals. PRESENTATION TO MRS. DOWNES. Mrs. Downes, daughter of Mr. Blair, Market-street. Earlestown, was the recipient of some very handsome presents on the eve of her departure for Australia. Mrs. Downes was formerly a teacher at the Earlestown Wesleyan Schools, but became head mistress at the Nutgrove Wesleyan Schools, St. Helens, which post she has held for some years. The managers gave her a gold chain and pendant and the scholars and staff a case of silver-backed brushes and mirror. Mrs. Atkinson, on behalf ofthe ladies' class of the Earles- town Wesleyan S.S., presented her with a travelling clock.

Edwards, F.C.S., London, last evening addressed a gathering of educationists and others ; concerned in the public health at the Technical ! Institute. The meeting had been arranged by the Warrington Band of 'lope Union and was largely attended, the subject of the address being "Suggestive methods of teaching hygiene and temperance in the schools."Mr.Ed wards is a leading scientist, the author of many standard health books, and a demonstrator who has few equals. Latterly in connection with his work of Director of the Department of Scientific Education and Information U.K.B.H.U. he has visited the chief centres of England, Ireland, Scotland and Wales, has addressed many Colleges and large personally invited gatherings of ladies, medical men, scientists, primary and secondary school teachers, and members of Health and Education Committees in various parts of the country. His visit to this district had been arranged by the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union to give those interested in child life an opportunity to meet and confer with him, and to hear what he had to say on health and kindred subjects. Mr. J. Upson presided.—Mr. Edwards said he was not asking the teachers to undertake a temperance propaganda. He advocated the teaching in the schools of the ordinary principles of life and health and the incorporation of scientific temperance teaching, pointing out how this could be done without inter- ferance with the school curriculum.—During the even- ing musical items were rendered by Miss Mabel Brown and Miss M. Hackett. Refreshments were served at the close of the proceedings.

Warrington Bandsmen's Success. Eleven bands competed on Saturday at the band contest at Batley, at which the baritone and soprano special prizes were awarded to Messrs. N. Snape and A. Allinson, respectively, both members of Crosfield's band. Wife Desertion. Annie Gill, 19, Orchard-street, Warrington, was, at the Police Court yesterday morning, granted a separation order, with an allowance of 12s. a week, from her husband, George Gill, Ernest-street, Altrincham, he having deserted her. Complainant said her husband left her five weeks ago, and had not given her any money since. It was stated that the defendant was " a bad lot." Coal Stealing. Catherine Ward, 1, Litherland's-yard, Warrington, was fined ss. at the Police Court, yesterday morning, for stealing nine-penny-worth of coal from A rpley New Siding, the property of the London and North- Western Railway Company. Mr. Smythe, who pro- secuted, asked for the assistance of the magistrates, as many thefts took place, and people exposed them- selves to danger. Suicide at Warrington. The body of David Wilkins, 14, Sutton-street, Warrington, was discovered at nine o'clock on Thurs- day night hanging in an out-house at the rear of his house. The shocking discovery was made by his wife and it appears that the deceased, who was a wood turner, had been depressed for some time. The body was taken to the police mortuary after P.C. Cox had tried artificial respiration. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER TIM CLOCK FOR BBST BOOTS IN LANCA.SHIRIL—Advt. Theft by a Railway Porter. Joseph Bennett, a rail way porter in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway at Warring- ton, pleaded guilty at the Borough Court, yesterday morning to two charges of theft. At 2.30 in the morning Police Constable Edwards discovered the prisoner stealing timber from a waggon on the Dallam-lane Siding, and on a search being made at the house in Green-street a brush belonging to the railway company was found. A fine of 203. was imposed in each case. " Doing" the Railway Company. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, yesterday morning, before Alderman Tinnion and other magis- trates, Edwin Norbury, 55, Hardshaw-street, St. Helens, was charged with travelliag on the London and North-Western Railway on May 11, June Ist and Bth, without having previously paid his fare.- Mr. Smythe, who prosecuted, said that it was a deliberate attempt on the part of the defendant to defraud the railway company. On two of the dates in question defendant travelled from St. Helens, via Widnes, to Thelwall, and on reaching the latter place he tendered 2d., the fare from Latchford to Thelwall. On another occasion he travelled to Lymm, and on passing through the gate said " Contract," whereas he did not hold a contract.—Mr. Tickle, who appeared for the defendant, said unfortunately when defendant had some drink he did not know what he was doing.—Defendant was ined 20s. and costs (£1 7s. 6d.) in each case. GENERAL KUROKI VISITS CHICAGO STOCK YARDS.

General Kuroki, the celebrated Japanese Field Marshal, visited the Packing Plants at Chicago a few days ago and was well pleased with the perfection of American packing house methods. Speaking of his visit he said :—" I have passed a morning of unusual interest. I found in the Stockyards cleanliness to a greater degree than I expected. The magnitude of busi- ness and the vast quantities of products handled astonished me. I sax there some corned beet—the same kind that I ate in Manchuria—and I was glad to have had an opportunity to see how the meat that the Japanese soldiers ate was prepared." He saw the Hog-killing at Swift 8; Company's, the canning at Libby, McNeill 6: Libby's, the offices and sausage room of the Morris Beef Company's plant, and the killing and dressing of beef and the wholesale , market at Armour's.

General Kuroki was specially interested in the United States Government inspection of meats and methods of seal tight packing of products for shipment to the Orient.

KILLED BY A THORN AT 95.

John Wilson, of Penge, ninety-five years old, was picking gooseberries on luesday, when he ran a thorn into his finger, and, although the thorn was extracted, he died of blood poisoning within two hours.

NEW MAN AT BOLD-STREET CHURCH.

OPENING BY DR. KENDRICK PYNE,

It has long been the desire of the congregation of Bold-street Wesleyan Church to possess an organ adequate to the needs of such a large and important church, and Thursday saw the consummation of many months' cheerful effort to attain that object. A magni- ficent instrument has been built and on Thursday 'evening the dedication and opening took place. By reason of its wonderful mechanical appliances the organ may certainly be regarded as unique locally Although there are only two manual keyboards, it is possible to obtain the full effects of three manuals and with considerably more rapidity than if three key- boards were at the service of the performer. Some idea of the completeness of the accessory movements may be gathered from the fact that whereas the speak- ing stops and couplers number in all thirty-one, the accessories carry the total number of items ' to fifty-nine, thereby giving twenty-eight devices for the prompt and efficient manipulation 'of the stops composing the specification. IThe organ has been supplied by Moasi.. Minato.), aria Foster, of Sheffield, at d cost of £9OO. The action of the instrument is thelatest form of tubular pneumatic, constructed according to the recent patented inventions of Mr. Charles Brindley, and it is electrically blown, the cost of current being only one penny per hour. The organ is enclosed in a very handsome case and its general appearance adds much to the dignity and beauty of the church. It will be remembered that Mr. Andrew Carnegie promised £430 towards the cost of the organ and his generous offer stimulated the workers who are to be cordially congratulated upon securing such a splendid adjunct to the services of the church as this instrument, which has been admired and appreciated by all who have had the privilege of hearing it. The committee (Rev. J. Manning, Messrs. F. Pendlebury, F. Hickman, and G. E. Guest) have certainly made an admirable choice and it must be . gratifying to them to know that the outcome of their long and devoted work in the matter has been so eminently satisfactory. The committee were fortunate in securing theservices ' of such a famous organist as Dr. J. Kendrick Pyne, of . Manchester, for the opening recital. The doctor holds ' the appointments of organist at the Cathedral and University and to the Corporation of Manchester and has a great reputation as a performer of classical:works. There was a representative gathering, including many of the musical fraternity, and the attendance would doubtless have been larger had not the weather been so . favourable to outdoor enjoyment. The Rev. J. Manning conducted a brief service and was supported on the rostrum by the members of the committee and a large number of circuit officers. The hymn "All people that on earth do dwell" having been sung, a dedicatory prayer was offered by the Rev. J. Manning, who after- wards delivered a short address. He congratulated the congregation upon that happy occasion, which was to them as a Church unique. They were realizing the hopes which had been cherished for some years by those interested in the musical services of that sanctuary. In opening the new organ they did not forget the old one. He had had the pleasure of handling the programme submitted on the occasion of the opening of the old instrument fifty years ago by Dr. Beat. They parted with that organ with a great deal of regret, for in spite of its faults a great deal of sentiment had gathered round it. They were glad to know that though it served them no longer, it was serving another Church in the neighbourhood, and its days would probably yet be many. He thought he might venture to say that after they had heard Dr. Kendrick Pype's recital they would agree that they had now an instrument which was worthy of the place and one that was thoroughly adequate to the services held there. They as a people believed that in that department of service as in every other. they should give the best they could afford. They believed nothing was too good for the House of the Lord. During the past few years the congregation hadbeen working and giving most generously to make that sanctuary fitting to their ideas of what it should be for the service of God. He was sure their friends in the choir would feel a great stimulus now that this fine instrument was at their disposal. The musical part of the services while he had been at Bold-street had always been good. He trusted that not only the choir but the congregation also would appreciate what had been done in the matter and that that House of Prayer would be even more helpful and that the glory of that place in the days to come would be greater than its glory in the past. Dr. Kendrick Pyne then proceeded with his recital, the programme being as follows : Slow Movement in D major ... ... Beethoven. Hymne Autrichien, varied, in G major ... ... Haydn. Suite in D major, for Strings and Bass... G. F. Handel. (Marche. Largo. Gavotte. Minuette. Rondeau. Marche.) Allegretto Cantabile, from the sth Symphony... "Vidor. Grand Prelude and Fugue in E minor ... J. S. Bach. Melody, for Violoncello and Pianoforte Pyne. sear.' Morceau Religieuse... ... ... ... ... Grim. , Finale in A major, for the Organ ... ... Salome. Each work was given in masterly style, Dr. Pyne dis- playing perfect technique and great taste in his inter- pretations. He brought out to the full the exceptionally beautiful qualtities of tone of the instrument as well as its fine power, and the recital gave manifest pleasure to all who heard it. The " Prelude and Fugue in E. Minor" was a brilliant performance for which Dr. Pyne was warmly applauded. The range of the pro- gramme served to demonstrate his abilities in varying styles of music and in each he was equally successful. The programme was very pleasingly varied by the in.' trodnction of two vocal solos by Mrs. Arthur Booth who sang "My heart ever faithful (Bach) and" With. verdure clad' (Haydn) with expression, her rich voice being heard to distinct advantage. The opening services will be continued on Sunday, when the preacher will be the Rev. Samuel T. House, of Liverpool. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., will preside at the organ, and at the close of the evening service will give a recital.On Thursday next there will be recitals at 3.30 and 7.30 by Mr. David Clegg, of Crystal Palace and Queen's Hall, London, who will submit very attrac- tive programmes including famous band contest selec- tions and "The Storm piece "(Moritz).

OUR NEW SERIAL. ROMANTIC AND SENSATIONAL ,TORY BY AN AUTHOR POPULAR

IN BOTH HEASPHERES.

WE have pleasure in announcing that we have arranged for the publication in our columns of a new Story entitled—

" rEI E VERDICT OF Tag El EMIT."

BY CHARLES GARV ICE.

AUTHOR OF "HER HEART'S DESIRE," "IN CUPID'S CHAINS," "JUST A GIRL," " NANCE," "THE OUTCAST OF THE FAMILY," "A CORONET OF SHAME," ETC.

Lovers of powerfully-written, sensational fiction should look out for the opening chapters of this new story, which will prove one of the • most deeply interesting yet published in this journal.

SOMETHING ABOUT THE AUTHOR..

M. CHARLES GARVICE, the author of our new story, belongs to the robust school of literary men. His Devonshire neighbours know him as a gentleman farmer, a keen lover of rural life, and punctilious in the fulfilment of his duties as County Councillor, Conservator of Rivers, and the like. His country habits, however, only give him the more time for the hard work of authorship, to which he devotes himself with characteristic goodwill. Some- thing of his vital energy, too, he is able to impart to his stories, with which he has been uniformly successful. "Just a Girl" has passed its hundred thousand, and his other books have been taken as quickly as he can write them. It is a tribute to his vigour and directness of style that his works circulate in America far more widely than those of any other living English novelist.

"T a VERDICT OF lE HEART,"

BY CHARLES GARVICE.

===== 0002601_19070713_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
SOMETHING ABOUT THE STORY.

Mr. Garvice always delineates his hero and heroine with great care. The" woman of the story," in fact, he considers its most important element. In "THE VERDICT OF THE HEART," the rble is assumed by Veronica Denby, proud and beautiful, the niece of the aged Earl of Lynborough. Veronica has known poverty— her father's death was hastened by actual want —but she has been adopted by the Earl, and rules his household. The Earl, indeed, lets her know that he has left her his whole realisable wealth, his nephew, Talbot Denby, succeeding only to the title and entailed estates. The condition is that she will wed only a man of title, and Veronica proudly assents. It requires, therefore, all the author's skill to make it seem probable that she would stoop to love a gamekeeper on her uncle's estate. Ralph Farringdon, however, is no ordinary man. He has recently come from Australia, and gives only vague accounts of his life there. He has good looks unquestionably, and he also shows good breeding. The reader is aware of a mystery concerning his parentage. He has a powerful rival in Talbot Denby, who badly needs the heiress's money to retrieve gambling losses, and Veronica fights hard against what she considers an unworthy predilection. But after Ralph has given undoubted evidence of nobility of soul, the verdict of the heart pre- vails, and the patrician leaves gladly the splendours of Lynborough Court for an uncertain future without.

The rivalry between the two men, however, leads to exciting events. The secret of Ralph's origin comes to Talbot's notice. It must be suppressed at all costs, and murder is one of the means adopted, the crime being first laid to Ralph's charge. At these illadents, whinh make en really the most absorbing part of the story, and at the surprising denouement. . we can do no more than hint. The reader wilt find the whole work one of unusual attraction.

" THE VERDICT OF THE HEART,"

BY t CHARLES GARVICE.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. EVERY DOCTOR HAS HIS _ FAVOURITE. The Church of To-Day. Rev. John Yonge and the Attitude of ' the Masses. The Church—the Body of Christ" was the subject .I.li a sermon by the Rev. John Yonge at Wycliffe Church „n Sunday evening. Taking for his text the words , The Church. which is His Body" (Ephesians i., 22, 23), he said that word of St. Paul bade them to understand the essential design and purpose of -the Church as being the outward, visible' tangible" body "possessed by the Spirit, of Christ for the fulfilment and accomplishment Of His will. As he saw it succinctly phrased the other in an article upon "The Living Church "—" The Church is called the' body of Christ '—His eyes, His 'Y °ice, His hands, His feet '—the living witness of God p this world of ignorance and sin and sorrow. The usual 'Elea of the Church had been far more that of "e_nremiinity in formal belief or in methods of rrship, of prayer or of praise, of ceremony or of ng• But. the Church of the New Testament Ms that ~(:) °,ramenity—be its name,creod and ceremony what they "Al/it—which could he "His Body "—His eyes, His voice, His hands, His feet, and, might they not add, as e inmost secret of it all, His heart. Men were now-a-days very largely holding off from ',rite Christian Church. After nineteen centuries of the „,'`nistian era, the actual Church of Christ in all the 'Mid was in a tremendous minority. It was so even in p'"entries called Christian. It was so in our own land. they might form any correct opinion from when r„: ;,'ght be seen of the religious observances of that TrE,s°Ple, the Church of all sects was assuiedly not grow-, fg, in proportion to the population, even if it be not tring ground. This too patent fact might well make • neghtful Christian people seriously reflect. How came It that the Church—meant to be " The Body of Christ" (t),n earth—did not draw mankind to itself? Why were 4e masses ontsiae the Sanctuaries ? Whydid the masses 6, the people practically ignore and reject organized i:"rietianity ? When Jesus in His physical body was flocked among men, the Gospels told them how the people irked to Him, and pursued after Him, waiting upon H.lB teaching, upon His working, and so intensely that pere was a time whey the) would have" taken Him by I,°Tee to make Him a King." But now that His Church had been set up for 1900 years and had been carried titarly all over the world, He won fewest victories where 15 Church had been longest known and was most fully Ilet up. They got tidings from afar, at times, which tnetified that He had not lost His ancient saving power ut here at home, and in other so-called Christian lands the stories of triumph were comparatively, rare and the thought still stood without. Anyone who read and "light upon the common facts of the attitude of the nesses to the Gospel, was faced and shamed by this ;19,0fness of men from the Church. What was the tconetion of the problem? He confessed that it seemed -or hiln it might be discoverable in this—that speaking t the common condition of the organized Church of 0-dsy, it was not realising its ideal, even approxi- nlatelv. The ideal was that the Church was the Body Christ—His eyes, His voice' His hands, His feet. he Church, therefore, should be doing now what Jesus Zae doing in His three years' ministry—serving tintnanity in every possible and conceivable way. Did 46 Church of all the sects come up to the IDattern given in His earthly life? Had it eyes vt°,see the needs of men as men; had it a to declare to all classes the message et the times ; bad it hands ready to serve; had it I et willing to stand and walk, and even run upon the I .I'Llieds of mercy to needy and helpless mankind? ,511 e are driven " said the writer of the article from ieh he had already quoted—" We are driven by the of life to a definition of the Church which ' Mbraces the interests of the whole world, and which ~xleludes every beneficent form of human activity— is, in short, the whole of human society organized. Or the spiritual ends of man." He (the preacher) under- Stood ; the writer to mean thdt the Church of every name ~ ! it Anglican, Roman, Nonconformist—was to begin I ce-ring infinitely less- about forms and creeds, about r,rnony and ritual, about prestige and place power, and infinitely more about human well- tbing in the broadest possible sense. He meant ! t the Church of every name and kind was provide the Spirit of the Saviour with a 10°Q13' in and by which He could move and work trng all the people: through which He could remedy emyriad ills of human life, comfort its terrible Sorrowsa, redeem men from their wrongs and vices and j ...Int. In a word, the Church of Jens was to be the jp, 'um of His blessed Spirit, seeking human salvation i "every possible sense. Now was that what the "nrch was in this present day? It might be what it 140 fessed to he and what it even claimed to be but the restion was" Does the Church commend itself in that 411 to all sorts and conditions of men ? " Was the I "ern Church, in any true, real sense, His Body?

THE ART MASTERSHIP. Mr. G. S. Pendlebury's Position. Since our reference last week to the vacant Art Mastership and Mr. G. S. Pendlebury's position in regard thereto, the matter has aroused considerable local interest. It will be remembered that at a meet- ing on June 21st the School Management Committee resolved that Mr. Clarence Mawson, A.R.C.A. (of Shipley) be appointed Headmaster of the School of Art at a salary of £225 per anuum together with certain grants on papers worked by the students. Mr. Mawson was selected from the following candidates who appeared before the Committee, viz.: -Mr. George C. Duxburv, Mr. Wilson Jagger, Mr. Clarence Mawson, Richard Macintosh, Mr. Frederic Paul, Mr. William Parkinson, Mr. Alfred J. Rushton, and Mr. G. H. Wood. Mr. Pendlebury, the assistant-master of the School of Art, was one of the applicants for the post, but the Committee excluded him from the list of those selected to appear before them "in view of the terms of para- graph 46 of the Board of Education's Regulations for Schools of Art." Mr. Pendlebury at once wrote to the Board of Education, and received a reply which stated "that the certificates which you hold are recognised as sufficient for the principal teacher of a School of Art under chapter 4 of the regulations for Technical Schools, Schools of Art, etc., 1907-03." Following upon this official reply, Mr. Pendlebury wrote to the School Management Committee requesting that his testi- monials be read and the whole matter re-considered. The letter was submitted at its meeting on July 4th and a reply sent to Mr. Pendlebury on July Bth stating that they could not see their way to do any- thing further in the matter." There is no record, either of Mr. Pendlebury's letter or the reply thereto on the minutes of the committee for 'that date, which have been printed and issued for consideration at the meeting of the full committee on Monday evening. These are briefly the facts of the case, which we hope will occupy the serious attention of the Educa- tion Committee on Monday. Mr. Pendlebury's application merits fuller consideration. N9RTHERN UNION. Mr. H. Ashton Elected President. At a meeting of the Northern Union General Com- mittee at Huddersfield on Tuesday, Mr. H. Ashton, secretary of the Warrington Club, was elected President of the Union. Other officials appointed were :—Vice- president, Mr. J. Nicholl, Halifax ; hon. secretary, Mr. J. Platt, Oldham; and hon. treasurer, Mr. J. Nicholl, Halifax. Permission was granted to Leeds and Barrow Clubs to play a six aside contest. The Broughton Rangers Club was granted permissi9n to make a charge at the gates of trial games during August, the proceeds to be given to charity. THE NEW ZEALAND VISIT. The venues for the New Zealand matches were fixed as follows : v. Northern Union, at Leeds, January 25. v. England, at Wigan, January 11. v. Cumberland, at Workington, January 8. CUP TIE DATES. The following dates were fixed for the cup-ties proper :—First round, February 29th • second round, March 14th; third round, March 281 ; semi-finals. April 11th ; final, April 25th. The following clubs were exempted from the qualify- ing round of the cup-ties: Leeds, Wakefield Trinity, Hull, Hunslet, Bradford, Keighley, Halifax, Hudders- field, Salford, Batley, Leigh, Warrington, Broughton Rangers, Kingston Rovers, Rochdale Hornets, Bramley, Dewsbury, Oldham, Runcorn, St. Helens, Swinton, Wigan, Barrow, Millom, Widnes, and York. The Cup Committee were selected as follows: Messrs H. Ashton, J. H. Houghton, J. W. Wood. B. Croke, J. Nicholl, J. Platt and G. Taylor. NEXT SEASON EXTENDED. The committee granted the application of the Northern League to play next season's final on May 2nd, the reason for such application being owing to the visit of the New Zealand team, of the Easter holidays coming after the completion of the first stage of the League competition. LANCASHIRE COUNTY CUP. A meeting of the Lancashire County Union was held at the Grosvenor Hotel, Manchester, on Friday, Mr. H. Ashton, Warrington presiding, Mr. Collinge, Rochdalei was appointed president. The draw for the first round of the Lancashire Senior Cup, to be decided on October 12, was made as follows : Broughton Rangers v. Rochdale Hornets. Runcorn v. Warrington_ Swinton v. St. Helens. Oldham v. Barrow. Byes—Salford, Wigan, Leigh and Widnes. The venus of County matches were fixed as follows : October s—Lancashire v. Cumberland, at the Broughton Rangers ground. January 18—Lancashire v. New Zealand, at Oldham. The cup tie dates for Lancashire Junior Cup were fixed as follows :—First round, January 4; second round, January 18; third round, February 1; fourth round, February 15 ; final, April 20. In Memoriam. In loving memory of Avice, the dearly loved wife of William Beswick, who fell asleep July 9th 1903. When she had passed, it seemed Like the ceasing of exquisite music. HUSBAND AND CHILDREN. • ADA AI S.—ln sad but ever loving memory of Thomas Charles (Tommy), the beloved son of Lewis George and Sarah Ann Adams who died July Sth, 1900, at 121, Legh at., Earlestown. We shall hear his voice in the Lamb's bright Hall, Hear his feet in the streets of geld, And the same bright smile on his glorified face Will speak of our Tommy of old. Our life is but a fading dawn, Its glorious noon how quickly past; Lead us 0 Christ when all is gone Safe home at last, Where Thou eternal light of light Art Lord of all. MOTHER.
